\section{The counts and the main results}\label{tcn:f:sec:results}
\subsection{Original combinatorial conventions}
Fix an integer $d\ge2$. Throughout this Part, $d$ is fixed as $n\to\infty$; constants in bounds may depend on $d$. Use the conventions of Chang, Fuchs, Liu, Wallner, and Yu \cite{Chang2024}. A network is a simple directed acyclic graph with one root of indegree zero and outdegree one, $n$ leaves of indegree one and outdegree zero bijectively labelled by $[n]$, and internal vertices of either indegree one and outdegree two (tree vertices), or indegree $d$ and outdegree one (reticulations). There are no vertices of indegree and outdegree one. The tree-child condition requires every nonleaf vertex to have at least one child that is not a reticulation. Networks are counted up to isomorphism preserving leaf labels; no plane ordering is imposed.

Write $T^{(d)}_{n,k}$ for their number with $k$ reticulations. The maximum is $k=n-1$; the maximal and total counts are $T^{(d)}_{n,n-1}$ and $T^{(d)}_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}T^{(d)}_{n,k}$. These are general tree-child networks, rather than the one-component subclass in which each reticulation is directly followed by a leaf.

The relevant words use $n$ ordered letters $\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_n$, each occurring $d+1$ times. In each prefix, if the count of $\omega_i$ exceeds $d-2$, that count must be at least the count of every $\omega_j$ with $j>i$. Let $a_n$ denote the number of these words, and set $a_0=1$. Equivalently they are the rectangular $(d+1)\times n$ Young tableaux with the wall convention in \cite[Remark 3.3]{Chang2024}, with vertical walls in all but the first two rows. Their exact recurrence, from \cite[(17)]{Chang2024}, is \eqref{tcn:d:brec}. \emph{[Write phase: source 104 prints the same display with $b(n,m)$ for $b_{n,m}$ and $c_d(n)$ for $a_n$; it is printed once, in Part~I.]} The index $d$ on $b$ is suppressed, but never varied within a recurrence. The exact network bijection gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:maximalidentity}
 T^{(d)}_{n,n-1}=n!\,a_{n-1}.
\end{equation}
It is essential to keep the startup $b_{1,1}=1$: a convenient rescaling for a two-sided order estimate would change the amplitude here.

\subsection{The canonical asymptotic scale}
Let $z=z_1>z_2>\cdots$ be the negative zeros of the Airy function $\Ai$, $z=-2.338107410459767\ldots$. With the host's $\Lambda,\Gamma,c,\ell,B,\rho,\alpha$ of Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:results}, define further
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:constants}
 Q_\Gamma=\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}\Gamma(1+i/d_+),\qquad
 \delta_f=\frac{d_-d}{2d_+},\qquad\zeta_f=\frac{3d_-}{2d_+},
\end{equation}
so that $B/3=z(d_-/d_+)^{2/3}$ and
\[
 \rho=\delta_f-d_-+\zeta_f-\frac12=-\frac{d_-^2+d_-+2}{2d_+},\qquad
 \alpha=\rho-d_-=-\frac{d(3d-1)}{2(d+1)}.
\]
Here $\Gamma$ is the exponential base, not the unknown amplitude. Source 104 chose a separate letter for it to avoid confusing it with the notation $\gamma(d)$ for that base in \cite[Theorem 1.8]{Chang2024}; Part~I's $\Gamma=4\Lambda$ serves the same purpose.

\begin{theorem}[Positive amplitude and all orders; source 104]\label{tcn:f:thm:main}
For each fixed integer $d\ge2$ there is a canonically determined $\gamma_d\in(0,\infty)$ and a compatible sequence $C_{d,j}$, $C_{d,0}=1$, such that for every fixed $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:allorders}
 a_n=\gamma_d(n!)^{d-1}\Gamma^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^\rho
 \left(\sum_{j=0}^J C_{d,j}n^{-j/3}+O(n^{-(J+1)/3})\right).
\end{equation}
The first coefficient is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:c1}
 C_{d,1}=\frac{z^2\bigl(8(d+1)^2-27(d-1)^2\bigr)}
 {90(d+1)^{4/3}(d-1)^{2/3}}
 =\frac{B^2\bigl(8(d+1)^2-27(d-1)^2\bigr)}{810(d-1)^2}.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 C_{2,1}=\frac{z^2}{2\,3^{4/3}}=\frac{B^2}{18},\qquad
 C_{3,1}=\frac{z^2}{36\,2^{1/3}}=\frac{B^2}{162},\qquad
 C_{d,1}<0\quad(d\ge4).
\]
All coefficients are effectively obtained by the exact polynomial algorithm in Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:formal}. They lie in $\Q[z,(d_-/d_+)^{1/3}]$ for fixed $d$.
\end{theorem}
This is a Poincar\'e expansion, meaning an asymptotic statement for each fixed finite depth. Neither convergence of the infinite formal series nor exponentially small terms is part of the theorem.

\begin{remark}[Relation to Part~I; write phase]\label{tcn:f:rem:main}
Theorem~\ref{tcn:f:thm:main} is Theorem~\ref{tcn:d:main} for the word counts, proved again by a different route; its $C_{d,j}$ are Part~I's $C_{d,r}$. The second form of \eqref{tcn:f:c1}, in the host's $B=3z(d_-/d_+)^{2/3}$, is a write-phase conversion: $B^2=9z^2(d_-/d_+)^{4/3}$, so the two forms agree identically. It reproduces the host's $C_{2,1}=B^2/18$ (Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:main}) and the first coefficient $B^2/162$ of \eqref{tcn:d:ternary}. The sign assertion holds because $8(d+1)^2-27(d-1)^2$ is $45$ at $d=2$, $20$ at $d=3$, $-43$ at $d=4$, and is a downward parabola in $d$ whose larger root is $(35+12\sqrt6)/19=3.389\ldots$; so $C_{d,1}<0$ exactly for $d\ge4$. Source 104's coefficient field $\Q[z,(d_-/d_+)^{1/3}]$ contains $B$; Part~I's statement $C_{d,r}\in\Q[B]$ (Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:transfer}) is sharper, and \eqref{tcn:f:c1} is visibly in $\Q[B]$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Maximal and total network equivalents; source 104]\label{tcn:f:cor:network}
For every fixed $d\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:maximalequivalent}
 T^{(d)}_{n,n-1}\sim\frac{\gamma_d}{\Gamma}(n!)^d\Gamma^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^\alpha.
\end{equation}
Its full Poincar\'e expansion follows by substituting $n-1$ in \eqref{tcn:f:allorders} and using \eqref{tcn:f:maximalidentity}. Its first correction is again $C_{d,1}n^{-1/3}$. Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:totalequivalent}
 T^{(d)}_n\sim K_d(n!)^d\Gamma^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^\alpha,
 \qquad K_d=\begin{cases}\sqrt\ee\,\gamma_2/12,&d=2,\\\gamma_d/\Gamma,&d\ge3.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
For $d=3$ specifically, with $B=3z/2^{2/3}$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:total3}
 T^{(3)}_n=\frac{\gamma_3}{32}(n!)^3 32^n
 \ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{-3}
 \left(1+\frac{z^2}{36\,2^{1/3}}n^{-1/3}+O(n^{-1/2})\right),
\end{equation}
where $z^2/(36\cdot2^{1/3})=B^2/162$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The factorial shift is $(n-1)!=n!/n$, the exponential-base shift is $\Gamma^{n-1}=\Gamma^n/\Gamma$, and the stretched-exponential shift first contributes at order $n^{-2/3}$. The polynomial shift first contributes at order $n^{-1}$. These prove the maximal claims. For totals, \cite[Corollary 1.11]{Chang2024} proves $T^{(2)}_n/T^{(2)}_{n,n-1}\to\sqrt\ee$ and $T^{(d)}_n/T^{(d)}_{n,n-1}\to1$ for $d\ge3$. The quantitative ternary assertion is proved in Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:ternarytotal}.
\end{proof}

The prior result \cite[Theorem 1.8]{Chang2024} already gives the full two-sided $\Theta$ scale in \eqref{tcn:f:maximalequivalent}, both for maximal and total counts. The positive limiting amplitude and the all-order maximal/word expansion are the additional assertions proved here. \emph{[Write phase: ``additional'' relative to the published literature; in the repository the same assertions are Theorem~\ref{tcn:d:main} of Part~I, proved independently from source 16 and printed first (Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:provenance}).]} The published ratio theorem alone would not give an all-order total expansion. For $d\ge4$ only the leading total equivalent is asserted in this Part.

\section{An exact normalization and a positive Jacobi product}\label{tcn:f:sec:exact}
\subsection{Rotating the recurrence without changing its startup}
Put $\widetilde b(p,q)=b_{p+1,q+1}$ on $0\le q\le p$, so that $\widetilde b(0,0)=1$. Subtract the successive prefix equations in \eqref{tcn:d:brec}. With forbidden inputs zero, the result away from the prescribed initial point is
\begin{align}
 \widetilde b(p,q)&=\mathcal W(p,q)\widetilde b(p-1,q)+\mathcal V(p,q)\widetilde b(p,q-1),\label{tcn:f:B}\\
 \mathcal W(p,q)&=\frac1{d_-!}\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}(dp+q+i),&
 \mathcal V(p,q)&=\frac{dp+q+d_-}{dp+q}.\notag
\end{align}
No expression with the zero denominator at $(0,0)$ is evaluated. The endpoint identity is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:endpointexact}
 a_n=\frac{\widetilde b(n,n)}{\mathcal W(n,n)},\qquad n\ge0,
\end{equation}
with the endpoint convention $\mathcal W(0,0)=1$. For $n\ge1$, this follows from $b_{n+1,n+1}=\mathcal W(n,n)a_n$.

Use the exact diagonal product
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:G}
 G_\Gamma(p)=\prod_{l=1}^p\mathcal W(l,l)
 =\Lambda^p\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}(1+i/d_+)_p,\qquad G_\Gamma(0)=1,
\end{equation}
where $(x)_p=\Gamma(x+p)/\Gamma(x)$. On the parity-compatible domain $0\le h\le N$, define
\[
 \mathcal Y(N,h)=\frac{\widetilde b((N+h)/2,(N-h)/2)}{G_\Gamma((N+h)/2)}.
\]
Then $\mathcal Y(0,0)=1$ and
\begin{align}
 \mathcal Y(N,h)&=\omega^-_N(h)\mathcal Y(N-1,h-1)+\omega^+_N(h)\mathcal Y(N-1,h+1),\label{tcn:f:onestep}\\
 \omega^-_N(h)&=\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}\frac{d_+N+d_-h+2i}{d_+(N+h)+2i},&
 \omega^+_N(h)&=\frac{d_+N+d_-h+2d_-}{d_+N+d_-h}.\notag
\end{align}
For instance $\mathcal Y(1,1)=\mathcal W(1,0)/\mathcal W(1,1)$ and $\mathcal Y(2,0)=1$, exactly.

\subsection{The two step matrix and its Gram factor}
Let $e_n(k)=\mathcal Y(2n,2k)$, $0\le k\le n$, and $e_0=\delta_0$. For fixed $n,k$ write $\mathfrak z=d_+n+d_-k$, $\mathfrak P=d_+(n+k)$, and define
\begin{align}
 \mathsf A_n(k)&=\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}\frac{\mathfrak z+i}{\mathfrak P+i},&
 \mathsf B_n(k)&=\frac{\mathfrak z+d_-}{\mathfrak z},\notag\\
 \mathsf C_n(k)&=\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}\frac{\mathfrak z+i-1}{\mathfrak P+i}=\frac{\mathsf A_n(k)}{\mathsf B_n(k)},&
 \mathsf F_n(k)&=\frac{\mathfrak z+d_--1}{\mathfrak z-1}.\label{tcn:f:coefficients}
\end{align}
Eliminating odd times in \eqref{tcn:f:onestep} gives $e_n=\mathsf M_ne_{n-1}$ after zero extension, where the lower, upper and diagonal entries are
\begin{align}
 \mathsf L_n(k)&=\mathsf A_n(k)\mathsf C_n(k-1),\notag\\
 \mathsf U_n(k)&=\mathsf B_n(k)\mathsf F_n(k),\label{tcn:f:matrixentries}\\
 \mathsf D_n(0)&=\mathsf B_n(0)\mathsf C_n(0)=\mathsf A_n(0)=1,\notag\\
 \mathsf D_n(k)&=\mathsf A_n(k)\mathsf F_n(k-1)+\mathsf B_n(k)\mathsf C_n(k)
 =\mathsf A_n(k)\left(1+\frac{\mathfrak z-1}{\mathfrak z-d}\right),\quad k\ge1.\notag
\end{align}
Use this full bulk diagonal also at the new coordinate $k=n$. It multiplies $e_{n-1}(n)=0$, so it leaves the exact evolution unchanged. It also avoids an artificial order-one jump at the old terminal coordinate when comparing consecutive matrices.

Set $\Sigma_n(0)=1$ and
\[
 \left(\frac{\Sigma_n(k)}{\Sigma_n(k-1)}\right)^2=\frac{\mathsf L_n(k)}{\mathsf U_n(k-1)}.
\]
Here $\Sigma_n$ also denotes the diagonal matrix with these entries. The symmetric matrix $J_n=\Sigma_n^{-1}\mathsf M_n\Sigma_n$ has diagonal $\mathsf D_n$ and positive off-diagonal entries
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:beta}
 \mathsf b_n(k)^2=\mathsf L_n(k)\mathsf U_n(k-1)
 =\mathsf A_n(k)\mathsf A_n(k-1)\frac{\mathfrak z-1}{\mathfrak z-d}.
\end{equation}
It is positive definite: $J_n=\mathsf K_n\mathsf K_n^{\mathsf T}$, with the square lower bidiagonal factor
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:gram}
 \mathsf K_n(k,k)^2=\mathsf A_n(k),\qquad
 \mathsf K_n(k,k-1)^2=\mathsf A_n(k)\frac{\mathfrak z-1}{\mathfrak z-d}\quad(k\ge1).
\end{equation}
Every displayed nonzero factor is positive. Let $\lambda_n$ be the largest eigenvalue of $J_n$, and $\psi_n$ its positive unit eigenvector, extended by zero in $\ell^2(\N_0)$. Put $\psi_0=\delta_0$.

\subsection{An exact contraction at every fixed degree}\label{tcn:f:sec:contraction}
Write $\varsigma_n(j)=\Sigma_n(j)/\Sigma_n(j-1)$. With $\mathfrak z=d_+n+d_-j$, its square factors as
\begin{align}
 \varsigma_n(j)^2={}&\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}\frac{\mathfrak z+i}{d_+(n+j)+i}
 \prod_{i=1}^{d_-}\frac{\mathfrak z-d+i}{d_+(n+j-1)+i}\notag\\
 &\hspace{25mm}\cdot\frac{\mathfrak z-d_-}{\mathfrak z}\,\frac{\mathfrak z-d}{\mathfrak z-1}.\label{tcn:f:edgefactor}
\end{align}
For $n\ge j\ge1$, every factor is in $(0,1]$. Denominator minus numerator equals, respectively, $2j$, $2j-1$, $d_-$, and $d_-$. Each numerator and denominator has the same positive slope $d_+$ as a function of $n$, so every factor increases with $n$. Thus $\Sigma_n(k)\le1$, and the zero-extended diagonal operator
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:R}
 \mathsf R_n(k)=\begin{cases}\Sigma_{n-1}(k)/\Sigma_n(k),&k<n,\\0,&k\ge n\end{cases}
 \quad\hbox{satisfies}\quad 0\le \mathsf R_n\le I.
\end{equation}
In particular $\mathsf R_n(0)=1$. On $n-1\le t\le n$ and $1\le j\le n-1$, all factors in \eqref{tcn:f:edgefactor} are bounded below by a positive constant depending only on $d$, and differentiation gives
\[
 0\le\partial_t\log \varsigma_t(j)\le C_d(j+1)/n^2.
\]
Integrate in $t$ and sum over $j\le k$. Enlarging the constant to include the new coordinate gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:Rdefect}
 0\le1-\mathsf R_n(k)\le C_d k(k+1)/n^2.
\end{equation}

This exact normalization is important at large fixed degrees. If $G_\Gamma(p)$ were replaced by $\Lambda^p(p!)^{d_-}$, its symmetrizer edge would instead satisfy, for fixed $j$,
\[
 \log(\varsigma_n(j)^2)=\frac{d_-(d_--4j)}{d_+n}+O(n^{-2}).
\]
When $d_->4$ and $j=1$, it decreases as $n$ increases, and the corresponding $\mathsf R_n(1)$ exceeds one for all sufficiently large $n$. Thus that simpler normalization does not prove contraction for $d\ge6$. Formula~\eqref{tcn:f:edgefactor} resolves the issue by an exact factorwise argument.

\begin{remark}[The obstruction does not touch Part~I; write phase]\label{tcn:f:rem:naive}
The obstruction concerns source 104's two-step frame with a factorial gauge. Part~I uses a different, one-step frame: its gauge ratio $Q_N=G_{N-1}/G_N$ is built from the weights $p_N(j)$ of \eqref{tcn:d:productp}, each of whose $d-1$ factors has the positive $N$-derivative displayed after \eqref{tcn:d:recover}, for every $d$. So $Q_N$ is a contraction at every fixed degree, and Part~I is unaffected. Part~IV's factorial gauge is the case $d=2$, where $d_-=1<4$ and no obstruction arises (Lemma~\ref{tcn:w:lem:R}).
\end{remark}

Finally let
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:product}
 P_n=\prod_{j=1}^n\lambda_j,\quad
 u_n=P_n^{-1}\Sigma_n^{-1}e_n,\quad
 \mathsf T_n=(J_n/\lambda_n)\mathsf R_n.
\end{equation}
Then $u_n=\mathsf T_nu_{n-1}$ and $u_0=\delta_0$. Each $\mathsf T_n$ is entrywise nonnegative and an $\ell^2$ contraction. The Gram factor proves positivity of the whole spectrum of $J_n/\lambda_n$, not just of its Perron eigenvalue.

\section{The Airy limit and quantitative spectral estimates}\label{tcn:f:sec:spectral}
\subsection{Global estimates and form compactness}
Put $\eps=n^{-1/3}$, $x_k=\eps(k+1/2)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:airyprofile}
 c_A=(4c)^{1/3}=(8d_-/d_+)^{1/3},\qquad
 f_A(x)=\frac{\sqrt{c_A}\Ai(z+c_Ax)}{\Ai'(z)}.
\end{equation}
Then $\int_0^\infty f_A^2\,dx=1$, $f_A'(0)=\sqrt{4c}$, and $f_A$ is the ground state of the Dirichlet operator
\[
 \mathcal H_A=-\frac{d^2}{dx^2}+4cx,\qquad
 \mathcal H_Af_A=-c_A^2zf_A.
\]
Let $J_\infty$ have diagonal $1,2,2,\ldots$ and unit off-diagonals. The exact finite-product coefficients satisfy
\begin{align}
 0<c_d&\le \mathsf b_n(k)\le C_d,\notag\\
 0\le J_n&\le(1+C_d/n)J_\infty\quad\hbox{entrywise},\label{tcn:f:global}\\
 4-\mathsf D_n(k)-\mathsf b_n(k)-\mathsf b_n(k+1)&\ge c_d k/n-C_d/n\quad(k\ge1),\notag\\
 4-\mathsf D_n(0)-\mathsf b_n(1)&=2+O(n^{-1}).\notag
\end{align}
Only existing edges enter these statements; a missing terminal edge is zero. To check them uniformly, put $\vartheta=(d_+n+d_-k)/(d_+(n+k))$. Then $d/d_+\le \vartheta\le1$ and
\[
 \mathsf A_n(k)=\vartheta^{d_-}+O(n^{-1}),\quad \mathsf D_n(k)=2\vartheta^{d_-}+O(n^{-1}),\quad
 \mathsf b_n(k),\mathsf b_n(k+1)=\vartheta^{d_-}+O(n^{-1})
\]
in the bulk. Extract $\vartheta$ from every factor; the remaining denominators are bounded below by a constant times $n$. Moreover
\[
 1-\vartheta^{d_-}\ge1-\vartheta=\frac{2k}{d_+(n+k)}\ge\frac{k}{d_+n}.
\]
The omitted terminal edge only increases the potential. Uniform positive bounds for the factors prove the edge bounds; $\mathsf A_n\le1$ and $(\mathfrak z-1)/(\mathfrak z-d)=1+O(n^{-1})$ prove the entrywise bound. Adjacent bulk coefficients differ by $O(n^{-1})$, also when the explicit expression for $\mathsf b_n$ is continued to the fictitious edge $n+1$.

On the common time domain, direct differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:timederivative}
 |\mathsf D_n(k)-\mathsf D_{n-1}(k)|+|\mathsf b_n(k)-\mathsf b_{n-1}(k)|
 \le C_d(k+1)/n^2.
\end{equation}
Here $\mathsf D_n(0)=1$ is exact, and the new edge is excluded.

The quadratic form of $4I-J_n$ is the weighted edge-gradient sum plus the potential in \eqref{tcn:f:global}. Its $n^{2/3}$-scaled low-energy limit is $\mathcal H_A$. We give the compactness details because the boundary and increasing dimension matter. For a unit vector of energy at most $C\eps^2$, the form bound implies
\[
 \sum_k k|u_k|^2\le C/\eps,\qquad |u_0|^2\le C\eps^2.
\]
Interpolate $u_k/\sqrt\eps$ linearly at $x_k$, start at zero at $x=0$, and add a one-mesh ramp to zero after $x_n$. The first half-cell derivative energy is $2|u_0|^2/\eps^2$, provided by the boundary potential tending to $2$. The other local derivative energies are bounded by the edge form. The first moment bounds the squared $L^2$ mass beyond $R$ by $C/R$. Local Rellich compactness together with this tail bound gives strong $L^2$ compactness, preserving norms and orthogonality. The negative potential correction is only $O(n^{-1})$ and disappears after scaling. Local coefficient convergence gives the gradient and potential lower limits. Sampled compactly supported smooth Dirichlet functions give matching upper bounds. \emph{[Write phase: this is the compactness argument of Remark~\ref{tcn:d:rem:compact} on the half-grid $x_k=\eps(k+\frac12)$, where the boundary half-cell replaces Part~I's fictitious first interval; Part~IV prints it at $d=2$ with a little more detail.]} The min--max principle therefore yields, for each fixed $j$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:spectrallimit}
 \frac{4-\lambda_n^{(j)}}{\eps^2}\longrightarrow-c_A^2z_j.
\end{equation}
In particular, positive definiteness and separation of the first two Airy levels give
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:gap}
 0\le\lambda_n^{(2)}/\lambda_n\le1-c_d n^{-2/3}.
\end{equation}
\emph{[Write phase: here the Gram factor excludes the negative half of the spectrum, so a full spectral gap holds, whereas Part~I's one-step matrix $S_N$ has a parity-symmetric spectrum and needs the parity singular gap of Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:spectrum}.]}

\subsection{The scalar term at order n inverse}
On the half-grid, the exact coefficients have expansions
\begin{align}
 \mathsf D_n(k)&=2-2cx\eps^2+\tfrac32c\eps^3
             +O(\eps^4(1+x^2)),\notag\\
 \mathsf b_n(k)&=1-cx\eps^2+\tfrac54c\eps^3
             +O(\eps^4(1+x^2)),\label{tcn:f:localcoeff}\\
 \mathsf b_n(k+1)&=1-cx\eps^2+\tfrac14c\eps^3
             +O(\eps^4(1+x^2)).\notag
\end{align}
For example, first expand $\mathsf A_n(k)=1-ck/n+O((k^2+k)/n^2)$ and $(\mathfrak z-1)/(\mathfrak z-d)=1+c/(2n)+O((k+1)/n^2)$, then use \eqref{tcn:f:beta} and \eqref{tcn:f:matrixentries}. \emph{[Write phase: source 104 writes these coefficients as $4r/a$, $3r/a$, $2r/a$, $5r/(2a)$, $r/(2a)$ and $r/(an)$, which are $2c$, $\tfrac32c$, $c$, $\tfrac54c$, $\tfrac14c$ and $c/(2n)$.]} The expansions are uniform for $k\le n/2$. Pairing $f_A(x+\eps)$ and $f_A(x-\eps)$ and using the adjacent-coefficient difference bound for odd derivative terms gives
\[
 J_nf_A=4f_A+\eps^2\left(f_A''-4cxf_A\right)
              +3c\eps^3f_A+O_{\ell^2}(\eps^4),
\]
where the samples include their factor $\sqrt\eps$. More explicitly, before that sampling factor the pointwise error is bounded by $C\eps^4$ times a fixed polynomial--Airy derivative envelope; its squared lattice sum times $\eps$ is bounded. The part $k>n/2$ is exponentially small.

At the first row, $\mathsf D_n(0)=1$ and $\mathsf b_n(1)=1-c\eps^3/4+O(\eps^6)$. The unsampled boundary residual is $O(\eps^4)$: the cubic term in $f_A(3\eps/2)-3f_A(\eps/2)$ cancels the eigenvalue term by
\[
 f_A(0)=f_A''(0)=0,\qquad f_A'''(0)=c_A^2zf_A'(0).
\]
Its sampled size is $O(\eps^{9/2})$. The chosen upper terminal diagonal contributes only an exponentially small error.

Let $\psi^A_n$ be the normalized sample $\sqrt\eps f_A(x_k)$. Midpoint quadrature gives pre-normalization squared norm $1+O(\eps^2)$. The residual estimate and isolation from \eqref{tcn:f:spectrallimit} identify the nearby eigenvalue as the largest one, and the gap gives
\begin{align}
 \lambda_n&=4+c_A^2z\,n^{-2/3}+3c\,n^{-1}
                                      +O(n^{-4/3}),\label{tcn:f:lambda}\\
 \norm{\psi_n-\psi^A_n}_2&=O(\eps^2).\label{tcn:f:profileapprox}
\end{align}
\emph{[Write phase: $c_A^2z=4^{2/3}\ell$. This agrees with two steps of Part~I's \eqref{tcn:d:lambda}: $(2+\ell N^{-2/3})^2=4+4^{2/3}\ell\,n^{-2/3}+O(n^{-1})$ at $N=2n$; the $n^{-1}$ terms differ because the gauges differ.]}

\subsection{Localization and changes in dimension}
Entrywise domination and positivity of $\psi_n$ give
\[
 \ip{\psi_n}{(4I-J_\infty)\psi_n}
 \le4-\frac{\lambda_n}{1+C_d/n}\le C\eps^2.
\]
The left side is $\sum_k|\psi_n(k+1)-\psi_n(k)|^2+2\psi_n(0)^2$. Discrete Sobolev therefore yields $\norm{\psi_n}_\infty\le C\sqrt\eps$.

Choose $k_A=\lceil A_0/\eps\rceil$ with $A_0$ large, and a fixed small $c_0>0$. The test function $w(k)=\exp[-c_0\eps(k-k_A)]$ is a strict supersolution $J_nw<\lambda_nw$ for $k>k_A$. Indeed, the full bulk row sum is at most $4-c_dk/n+C_d/n$. Continue the upper edge first to its fictitious value at $n+1$. The paired exponential shifts change that sum by $O(\eps^2)$; the first-order part uses the adjacent-coefficient difference $O(n^{-1})$. Comparing with \eqref{tcn:f:lambda}, choose $A_0$ large enough, then omit the positive fictitious contribution at $k=n$. The finite maximum principle gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:localization}
 \psi_n(k)\le C\sqrt\eps\,\ee^{-c_0\eps(k-k_A)}\quad(k\ge k_A),\qquad
 \norm{k^p\psi_n}_2\le C_p n^{p/3}\quad(p\ge0\text{ fixed}).
\end{equation}
In particular the old terminal component is exponentially small in $n^{2/3}$. Combining \eqref{tcn:f:timederivative} and \eqref{tcn:f:localization}, with the new edge estimated separately, gives
\[
 \norm{(J_n-J_{n-1})\psi_{n-1}}_2\le Cn^{-5/3}.
\]
Projecting this residual and using the gap proves
\begin{align}
 \norm{\psi_n-\psi_{n-1}}_2&\le C/n,\label{tcn:f:drift}\\
 \norm{(I-\mathsf R_n)\psi_n}_2+\norm{(I-\mathsf R_n)\psi_{n-1}}_2
                                      &\le Cn^{-4/3}.\label{tcn:f:Rperron}
\end{align}
The latter estimate is also an immediate consequence of \eqref{tcn:f:Rdefect} and the second moment in \eqref{tcn:f:localization}.

\subsection{Endpoint smoothing}
Let $\mathcal K=J_\infty/4$. The half-grid sine transform gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:kernel}
 \mathcal K^m(0,0)=\frac2\pi\int_0^\pi\cos^{2m}(u/2)\sin^2(u/2)\,du
 \le C(m+1)^{-3/2}.
\end{equation}
Hence $\norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}\mathcal K^l}_2\le C(l+1)^{-3/4}$. Positivity, $\mathsf R_j\le I$, \eqref{tcn:f:global}, and \eqref{tcn:f:lambda} imply, for $0\le l\le n^{2/3}$,
\begin{align}
 0\le \mathsf T_n\cdots \mathsf T_{n-l+1}&\le C\mathcal K^l,\notag\\
 \norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}\mathsf T_n\cdots \mathsf T_{n-l+1}}_2&\le C(l+1)^{-3/4}.\label{tcn:f:smoothing}
\end{align}
The accumulated comparison scalar is bounded because its logarithm is $O(ln^{-2/3})$. This is an entrywise product comparison and requires no commutativity. The same bound holds for powers of a fixed $J_n/\lambda_n$. \emph{[Write phase: this is Part~I's Catalan bound \eqref{tcn:d:smoothing} for the killed kernel $J_\infty/4$ in place of half-line adjacency.]}

For $\mathsf z=\psi_n-\psi^A_n$, the quasimode estimates give $\mathsf z=(J_n/\lambda_n)\mathsf z+\varrho$ with $\norm\varrho_2=O(\eps^4)$. Iterate $\Delta=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ times and apply \eqref{tcn:f:smoothing}:
\[
 |\mathsf z(0)|\le C\Delta^{-3/4}\eps^2+C\eps^4\sum_{l<\Delta}(l+1)^{-3/4}
 =O(\eps^{7/2}).
\]
Since $\psi^A_n(0)=(f_A'(0)/2)\eps^{3/2}(1+O(\eps^2))$, this proves the precise endpoint scale
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:perronendpoint}
 \psi_n(0)=\sqrt c\,n^{-1/2}\left(1+O(n^{-2/3})\right).
\end{equation}

\section{A strictly positive limiting amplitude}\label{tcn:f:sec:amplitude}
\subsection{Convergence of the normalized product}
Write
\[
 p_n=\ip{\psi_n}{u_n},\qquad
 p_n^\perp=\norm{(I-\psi_n\psi_n^{\mathsf T})u_n}_2.
\]
Contractivity gives $\norm{u_n}_2\le1$. The gap, drift, and symmetrizer defect give
\[
 p_n^\perp\le(1-cn^{-2/3})p_{n-1}^\perp+C/n.
\]
A power supersolution yields $p_n^\perp=O(n^{-1/3})$. The exact scalar increment is
\begin{align}
 p_n-p_{n-1}={}&p_{n-1}\bigl(\ip{\psi_n}{\mathsf R_n\psi_{n-1}}-1\bigr)\notag\\
 &+\ip{\mathsf R_n\psi_n-\psi_{n-1}}
 {(I-\psi_{n-1}\psi_{n-1}^{\mathsf T})u_{n-1}}.\label{tcn:f:scalarincrement}
\end{align}
The first bracket is $O(n^{-4/3})$: without $\mathsf R_n$ it is minus half the squared Perron-vector drift. The second term is $O(n^{-1}p_{n-1}^\perp)=O(n^{-4/3})$. Thus there exists $p_\infty\ge0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:productlimit}
 |p_n-p_\infty|+\norm{u_n-p_\infty\psi_n}_2\le Cn^{-1/3}.
\end{equation}

Convergence alone does not prove strict positivity. Restart the same exact normalized product at a large $N$ from the vector $\psi_N$. The preceding estimates hold with constants independent of $N$ above a fixed threshold; its initial perpendicular component is zero and its norm is one. Summing its scalar increments shows that its limiting Perron mass differs from one by at most $CN^{-1/3}$. Choose $N$ so large that this mass exceeds $1/2$. Every coordinate of the actual $u_N$ on $0,\ldots,N$ is strictly positive, by the original recurrence. Thus $u_N\ge\theta_0\psi_N$ for some $\theta_0>0$. Positivity of subsequent operators implies $p_\infty\ge\theta_0/2>0$.

Set $\mathsf z_n=u_n-p_\infty\psi_n$. Its exact evolution has forcing $p_\infty(\mathsf T_n\psi_{n-1}-\psi_n)$ of norm $O(n^{-1})$. Iterate through the last $\Delta=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps, use \eqref{tcn:f:productlimit}, then \eqref{tcn:f:smoothing}. The result is
\[
 |\mathsf z_n(0)|\le C\Delta^{-3/4}n^{-1/3}+\frac Cn\sum_{l<\Delta}(l+1)^{-3/4}
 =O(n^{-5/6}).
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:productendpoint}
 u_n(0)=p_\infty\sqrt c\,n^{-1/2}\left(1+O(n^{-1/3})\right).
\end{equation}
\emph{[Write phase: this is the restart comparison of source 104, not the contradiction with the lower $\Theta$ bound used in Part~I (Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:amplitude}) and Part~II; source 104's positivity proof therefore needs no external enumeration-asymptotic input.]}

\subsection{The exact gamma normalization}
Equation~\eqref{tcn:f:lambda} gives
\[
 \log(\lambda_n/4)=\frac B3n^{-2/3}+\zeta_fn^{-1}+O(n^{-4/3}).
\]
It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:Climit}
 C_\lambda=\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{P_n}{4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{\zeta_f}}\in(0,\infty),
\end{equation}
with relative error $O(n^{-1/3})$. A positive convergent representation is
\begin{align}
 C_\lambda={}&\frac{\lambda_1}{4}\ee^{-B}
 \prod_{j=2}^{\infty}\left[
 \frac{\lambda_j}{4}
 \ee^{-B(j^{1/3}-(j-1)^{1/3})}
 \left(\frac{j-1}{j}\right)^{\zeta_f}\right].\label{tcn:f:Cproduct}
\end{align}
Each factor is $1+O(j^{-4/3})$. Also, from $\ip{\psi_n}{\mathsf T_nu_{n-1}}=\ip{\mathsf R_n\psi_n}{u_{n-1}}$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:pseries}
 p_\infty=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}
 \ip{\mathsf R_n\psi_n-\psi_{n-1}}{u_{n-1}},
\end{equation}
and this scalar series is absolutely convergent by \eqref{tcn:f:scalarincrement}.

The exact product \eqref{tcn:f:G} has the asymptotics
\begin{align}
 G_\Gamma(n)&=\Lambda^n(n!)^{d_-} n^{\delta_f}Q_\Gamma^{-1}(1+O(n^{-1})),\notag\\
 \mathcal W(n,n)&=\Lambda n^{d_-}(1+O(n^{-1})),\label{tcn:f:gammaconversion}\\
 a_n&=\frac{G_\Gamma(n)}{\mathcal W(n,n)}e_n(0),\qquad e_n(0)=P_nu_n(0).\notag
\end{align}
The equality $\delta_f-d_-+\zeta_f-1/2=\rho$ now proves the leading instance of Theorem~\ref{tcn:f:thm:main}, with
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:gamma}
 \boxed{\displaystyle\gamma_d=
 \frac{\sqrt c\,p_\infty C_\lambda}{\Lambda Q_\Gamma}>0.}
\end{equation}
This fixes the amplitude for the exact original startup, including $a_0=a_1=1$. No closed form or certified numerical enclosure of $\gamma_d$ is asserted. The existence of a convergent defining product should not be confused with convergence of the all-order correction series proved next. \emph{[Write phase: \eqref{tcn:f:gamma} and Part~I's \eqref{tcn:d:gamma} are two convergent representations of one number, through different gauges; their factors $p_\infty$, $C_\lambda$ and $A_\infty$, $\kappa_d$ are not individually comparable.]}

\section{The all order coefficient algorithm}\label{tcn:f:sec:formal}
\subsection{An exact scaled one step equation}
Use a different Airy scaling for the one-step equation:
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:onescaling}
 t=N^{-1/3},\quad x=(h+1)t,\quad
 F(x)=\Ai(z+c^{1/3}x),\quad \eta_f=\zeta_f-\frac16.
\end{equation}
Then $F''=(cx+\ell)F$, $F(0)=0$, and $F'(0)=c^{1/3}\Ai'(z)\ne0$. The exact scaled weights of \eqref{tcn:f:onestep} are
\begin{align}
 U(t,x)&=\prod_{i=1}^{d_-}
 \frac{d_++d_-x t^2+(2i-d_-)t^3}{d_++d_+x t^2+(2i-d_+)t^3},\notag\\
 V(t,x)&=\frac{d_++d_-x t^2+d_-t^3}{d_++d_-x t^2-d_-t^3},\qquad
 t_1=t(1-t^3)^{-1/3}.\label{tcn:f:weights}
\end{align}
Seek formal series
\begin{align}
 s(t)&=2+\ell t^2+\sum_{j\ge3}\sigma_jt^j,\notag\\
 \mathfrak F(t,x)&=\sum_{j\ge0}t^jf_j(x),\qquad f_j=P_jF+Q_jF',\label{tcn:f:formalansatz}\\
 P_0&=1,\quad Q_0=0,\qquad
 Q_j(0)=0,\quad P_j(0)+Q_j'(0)=0\quad(j\ge1),\notag
\end{align}
satisfying
\begin{align}
 s(t)\mathfrak F(t,x)={}&U(t,x)\mathfrak F\bigl(t_1,(x-t)(1-t^3)^{-1/3}\bigr)\notag\\
 &+V(t,x)\mathfrak F\bigl(t_1,(x+t)(1-t^3)^{-1/3}\bigr).\label{tcn:f:formalequation}
\end{align}
For fixed $d$, the profiles $P_j,Q_j$ and scalars $\sigma_j$ have rational polynomial coefficients in $\ell,x$ or $\ell$. The first nontrivial equation is the Airy equation at order $t^2$.

Suppose earlier orders are solved. At order $t^k$, write the known residual as $R_0F+R_1F'$. The new profile has index $k-2$, and it and $\sigma_k$ must solve
\begin{align}
 P''+2(cx+\ell)Q'+cQ-\sigma_k+R_0&=0,\notag\\
 2P'+Q''+R_1&=0.\label{tcn:f:solveprofile}
\end{align}
Define the polynomial operator
\[
 \mathcal T=D^3-4(cx+\ell)D-2c.
\]
On polynomials of degree at most $m$, this operator is triangular with diagonal $-2c(2j+1)$, $0\le j\le m$. None is zero. Thus the following is an exact finite algorithm:
\begin{align}
 \widetilde q&=\mathcal T^{-1}(2R_0-R_1'),&
 \sigma_k&=-c\,\widetilde q(0),\notag\\
 Q&=\widetilde q+\sigma_k/c,&
 P(x)&=\int_0^x\frac{-R_1(y)-Q''(y)}2\,dy-Q'(0).\label{tcn:f:algorithm}
\end{align}
To verify it, eliminate $P''$ from \eqref{tcn:f:solveprofile}; this gives
$\mathcal TQ=2R_0-R_1'-2\sigma_k$. Substituting \eqref{tcn:f:algorithm} proves both equations and both gauges. Conversely the nonzero triangular diagonal and the gauges give uniqueness. This proves existence and compatibility to every formal depth. It also shows
\[
 f_j(0)=0\quad(j\ge0),\qquad f_j'(0)=0\quad(j\ge1).
\]
\emph{[Write phase: $\mathcal T$ is $-2$ times the left side of Part~I's triangular equation \eqref{tcn:d:triangular}; the polynomial inverse is the same.]}

For the first correction,
\[
 R_0=2c,\quad R_1=(c+2/3)x,\quad
 \sigma_3=3c/2-1/3=2\eta_f,
\]
so $Q_1=0$ and $P_1=-(3c+2)x^2/12$. The next two scalars are
\begin{align}
 \sigma_4&=\frac{\ell^2(189c^2-64)}{540c^2},\label{tcn:f:sigma4}\\
 \sigma_5&=\frac{\ell(351c^3-90c^2+64)}{324c^2}.\label{tcn:f:sigma5}
\end{align}
They are exact symbolic consequences of the recurrence, not numerical fits.

\subsection{Compatible scalar carriers}
Define $h_j$ by the formal difference identity
\begin{align}
 \log\frac{s(t)}2={}&\frac{3\ell}{2t}\bigl[1-(1-t^3)^{1/3}\bigr]
                    -\eta_f\log(1-t^3)\notag\\
 &+\sum_{j\ge1}h_jt^j\bigl[1-(1-t^3)^{-j/3}\bigr].\label{tcn:f:carrieridentity}
\end{align}
The coefficients at $t^2,t^3$ are $\ell/2,\eta_f$. At order $t^{j+3}$, the new unknown $h_j$ has nonzero coefficient $-j/3$. Thus all $h_j$ are uniquely determined, with no arbitrary additive constants. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:h1}
 h_1=\frac{3\ell^2}{8}-\frac{3\sigma_4}{2}
     =\frac{\ell^2(32-27c^2)}{180c^2}.
\end{equation}
\emph{[Write phase: the next carrier coefficient, used in Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:coefficients} but not printed by source 104, is $h_2=-\ell(27c^3-36c^2+16)/(108c^2)$, from \eqref{tcn:f:carrieridentity} and \eqref{tcn:f:sigma5} (computed symbolically in writing). At $d=2$, where $c=2/3$, $\sigma_4=\ell^2/12$, $\sigma_5=8\ell/9$, $h_1=\ell^2/4$ and $h_2=-\ell/6$.]}
For any fixed finite depth $M$, put
\begin{align}
 \mathcal H_N^{[M]}&=2^N
 \ee^{(3\ell/2)N^{1/3}}N^{\eta_f}
 \exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^Mh_jN^{-j/3}\right),\notag\\
 \mathfrak F^{[M]}(t,x)&=\sum_{j=0}^Mt^jf_j(x).\label{tcn:f:carrier}
\end{align}
Every carrier has leading multiplicative constant exactly one. Its exact consecutive ratio agrees with the formal $s$-series through at least order $t^{M+3}$; when needed the $h_j$ are computed using profiles beyond the retained profile depth.

At even times, write $N=2n$, $h=2k$, $\eps=n^{-1/3}$, and $X=\eps(k+1/2)$. Then $t=2^{-1/3}\eps$ and $x=2^{2/3}X$. Define
\begin{align}
 E_n^{[M]}(k)&=K_E\mathcal H_{2n}^{[M]}
 \mathfrak F^{[M]}(2^{-1/3}\eps,2^{2/3}X),\notag\\
 K_E&=\frac{\Lambda Q_\Gamma\,2^{1/3-\eta_f}}{F'(0)}.\label{tcn:f:Eprofile}
\end{align}
At $k=0$ the argument is $x=t$, and
\[
 \mathfrak F^{[M]}(t,t)=F'(0)t(1+O(t^2)).
\]
The derivative gauges ensure that higher profiles do not alter this leading endpoint normalization. Consequently every depth shares
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:Eleading}
 E_n^{[M]}(0)=\Lambda Q_\Gamma\,4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{\zeta_f-1/2}(1+O(\eps)).
\end{equation}

The exact conversion back to the counted sequence is crucial. Define
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:Z}
 Z^\Gamma_n=\frac{Q_\Gamma G_\Gamma(n)}{\Lambda^n(n!)^{d_-} n^{\delta_f}}\cdot
             \frac{\Lambda n^{d_-}}{\mathcal W(n,n)}.
\end{equation}
It tends to one and has a full expansion in integer powers of $n^{-1}$, from gamma-ratio asymptotics and the finite polynomial $\mathcal W(n,n)$. Its first term is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:Zfirst}
 Z^\Gamma_n=1+\left(\frac{d_-d(2d_-+1)}{12d_+^2}-\frac{d_-d}{4d_+}\right)n^{-1}
       +O(n^{-2}).
\end{equation}
The canonical multiplicative coefficient series is the expansion of
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:endpointseries}
 \frac{Z^\Gamma_nE_n^{[M]}(0)}
 {\Lambda Q_\Gamma\,4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{\zeta_f-1/2}}.
\end{equation}
The gamma constant $Q_\Gamma$ and $\Ai'(z)$ cancel here. The coefficients stabilize as the retained depth increases.

In particular, $Z^\Gamma_n$ has no $n^{-1/3}$ term and the endpoint profile has no term of order $t$. Hence $C_{d,1}=2^{-1/3}h_1$, which simplifies to \eqref{tcn:f:c1}. If $\omega^3=1$ and the Airy-equation solution is normalized by $\mathfrak B_\ell(0)=0$, $\mathfrak B_\ell'(0)=1$, then
\[
 \mathfrak B_{\omega\ell}(\omega x)=\omega\mathfrak B_\ell(x).
\]
The weights $U,V$ are invariant under simultaneous $(t,x,\ell)\mapsto(\omega t,\omega x,\omega\ell)$. Formal uniqueness preserves this grading. Thus a scalar monomial $\ell^mt^j$ in the normalized endpoint has $m+j$ divisible by three. On substituting $\ell=(2d_-/d_+)^{2/3}z$, $t=2^{-1/3}\eps$, the power $(2m-j)/3$ of $2$ is an integer. Together with the rational expansion of $Z^\Gamma_n$, this proves the coefficient field stated in Theorem~\ref{tcn:f:thm:main}.

\section{Transferring arbitrary formal depth to the exact counts}\label{tcn:f:sec:transfer}
\subsection{Residual estimates and the boundary}
For each fixed $M$ and $C_0$, the exact one-step residual of
$\mathcal H_N^{[M]}\mathfrak F^{[M]}(N^{-1/3},(h+1)N^{-1/3})$, divided by its scalar carrier, is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:oneresidual}
 C t^{M+3}(1+x)^D\ee^{-c_0x^{3/2}},\qquad 0\le h\le C_0\sqrt N,
\end{equation}
for constants depending on the fixed depth and degree. Here $c_0>0$ is a bound constant. In this region $x=O(t^{-1/2})$, so $xt^2=O(t^{3/2})$, and every denominator in the exact weights stays separated from zero. A finite rational Taylor expansion has a remainder bounded by the stated power of $t$ times a polynomial in $x$. The shifted arguments differ from $x$ by
\[
 \pm t+O(xt^3+t^4)=O(t).
\]
All derivatives of the finite polynomial--Airy profiles obey polynomial times $\ee^{-c_0x^{3/2}}$ bounds on those intervals. The exact finite-carrier ratio is analytic near $t=0$ after its constant $2$ is removed. Formal cancellation through degree $M+2$ gives \eqref{tcn:f:oneresidual}. This argument uses only finitely many terms, not convergence of an infinite profile series.

At the lower boundary $h=0$, the ghost predecessor $h=-1$ has scaled argument zero. Every finite profile vanishes there exactly, before Taylor expansion. The lower boundary is therefore exact. Eliminating the odd step gives precisely the even boundary diagonal $\mathsf D_n(0)=1$; the even residual is a bounded linear combination of one-step residuals.

Choose a smooth fixed cutoff $\chi$, equal to one on $[0,1/2]$ and zero on $[1,\infty)$, and let
\[
 \widetilde E_n^{[M]}(k)=\chi(k/\sqrt n)E_n^{[M]}(k).
\]
In the transition region $X$ is comparable to $n^{1/6}$, so the profiles and adjacent values are polynomial times $\ee^{-c_0n^{1/4}}$. Every cutoff error is therefore smaller than any fixed inverse power after removing the common scalar carrier. Include also the extra possible residual edge at $k=\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor+1$. From \eqref{tcn:f:edgefactor},
\[
 -\log \Sigma_n(k)=O((k^2+k)/n)=O(1),\qquad k\le\sqrt n+O(1).
\]
Thus $\Sigma_n^{-1}$ is uniformly bounded on the whole residual support.

Let $\Theta_n=4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{\eta_f}$. The squared polynomial--Airy envelope has lattice sum $O(\eps^{-1})$, so
\[
 \norm{\widetilde E_n^{[M]}-\mathsf M_n\widetilde E_{n-1}^{[M]}}_2
 =O(\Theta_n\eps^{M+5/2}).
\]
Since $\Theta_n/P_n=O(\eps^{1/2})$, we obtain the normalized defect
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:normalizeddefect}
 \norm{P_n^{-1}\Sigma_n^{-1}
  [\widetilde E_n^{[M]}-\mathsf M_n\widetilde E_{n-1}^{[M]}]}_2
 =O(\eps^{M+3})=O(n^{-(M+3)/3}).
\end{equation}

\subsection{The amplitude is the same at every depth}
Set $V_n^{[M]}=P_n^{-1}\Sigma_n^{-1}\widetilde E_n^{[M]}$. The leading coefficient in \eqref{tcn:f:Eprofile} and the normalized Airy sample give
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:normalmatch}
 \norm{V_n^{[M]}-\frac{\Lambda Q_\Gamma}{\sqrt c\,C_\lambda}\psi_n}_2
 =O(\eps).
\end{equation}
For the scalar calculation, $K_E2^{\eta_f}=\Lambda Q_\Gamma(d_+/d_-)^{1/3}/\Ai'(z)$; divide by the Airy normalization $\sqrt{c_A}/\Ai'(z)$ and include the half-power scalar ratio. The finite carrier correction and weighted profile correction are $O(\eps)$. On the cutoff support, $\Sigma_n^{-1}-1=O(\eps(1+X^2))$ in the polynomial--Airy weighted norm; the cutoff discards an exponentially small tail. Finally \eqref{tcn:f:profileapprox} has error $O(\eps^2)$. These justify \eqref{tcn:f:normalmatch} globally.

By the exact amplitude formula,
\[
 \gamma_d\frac{\Lambda Q_\Gamma}{\sqrt c\,C_\lambda}=p_\infty.
\]
Hence the signed error $\mathsf z_n=u_n-\gamma_dV_n^{[M]}$ has norm $O(n^{-1/3})$, limiting Perron projection zero, and exact evolution
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:forced}
 \mathsf z_n=\mathsf T_n\mathsf z_{n-1}+\varrho_n,\qquad
 \norm{\varrho_n}_2=O(n^{-p}),\qquad p=(M+3)/3.
\end{equation}
The profiles need not be positive. Positivity was used to prove the actual limiting amplitude; it is not assumed for the signed error.

\subsection{A forced product bootstrap}
\begin{lemma}\label{tcn:f:lem:forced}
Suppose \eqref{tcn:f:forced} holds with $p>4/3$, $\norm{\mathsf z_n}_2=O(n^{-1/3})$, and $\ip{\psi_n}{\mathsf z_n}\to0$. Then
\[
 \norm{\mathsf z_n}_2=O(n^{-p+1}),\qquad \mathsf z_n(0)=O(n^{-p+1/2}).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $a^{\rm err}_n=\ip{\psi_n}{\mathsf z_n}$ and $b^{\rm err}_n=\norm{(I-\psi_n\psi_n^{\mathsf T})\mathsf z_n}_2$. The gap, drift, and $\mathsf R$-defect estimates give
\begin{align}
 b^{\rm err}_n&\le(1-c_{\rm gap}n^{-2/3})b^{\rm err}_{n-1}+Cn^{-1}|a^{\rm err}_{n-1}|+Cn^{-p},\notag\\
 |a^{\rm err}_n-a^{\rm err}_{n-1}|&\le Cn^{-4/3}|a^{\rm err}_{n-1}|+Cn^{-1}b^{\rm err}_{n-1}+Cn^{-p}.\label{tcn:f:bootstrap}
\end{align}
If $|a^{\rm err}_n|+b^{\rm err}_n=O(n^{-q})$, the first inequality improves the transverse bound to
\[
 b^{\rm err}_n=O(n^{-q-1/3}+n^{-p+2/3}).
\]
This follows by a power supersolution for
$x_n\le(1-c_{\rm gap}n^{-2/3})x_{n-1}+O(n^{-s})$, which gives $x_n=O(n^{-s+2/3})$; any finite startup contribution decays as $\ee^{-c_2n^{1/3}}$. Substitute the improved transverse bound in the second inequality and sum from $n+1$ to infinity, using $a^{\rm err}_\infty=0$. Then
\[
 a^{\rm err}_n=O(n^{-q-1/3}+n^{-p+1}).
\]
All increments are absolutely summable: initially $q=1/3$ and $p>4/3$. Finitely many iterations improve the exponent by $1/3$ until reaching $p-1$, proving the norm estimate.

For the endpoint, iterate over the last $\Delta=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps and use \eqref{tcn:f:smoothing}:
\[
 |\mathsf z_n(0)|\le C\Delta^{-3/4}n^{-p+1}
       +Cn^{-p}\sum_{l<\Delta}(l+1)^{-3/4}
 =O(n^{-p+1/2}).
\]
\end{proof}
\emph{[Write phase: Lemma~\ref{tcn:f:lem:forced} is the zero-limit bootstrap \eqref{tcn:d:boot} of Part~I in source 104's frame; Part~IV's Lemma is the same statement at $d=2$.]}

The endpoint scale of $E_n^{[M]}$ is $4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{\zeta_f-1/2}$, whereas $P_n$ has power $\zeta_f$. Lemma~\ref{tcn:f:lem:forced} therefore gives relative error $O(n^{-p+1})=O(\eps^M)$ in $e_n(0)$, and after the exact common factor $G_\Gamma(n)/\mathcal W(n,n)$, also in $a_n$. To obtain the expansion through $\eps^J$, choose $M\ge J+1$ and $M\ge2$. Expand \eqref{tcn:f:endpointseries} through degree $J$; its next term is $O(\eps^{J+1})$ and the transfer error is at most that size. All coefficients come from compatible formal profiles, carriers and gamma factors, so they are independent of the deeper chosen truncation. This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{tcn:f:thm:main}.

\section{Explicit coefficients and total ternary networks}\label{tcn:f:sec:coefficients}
\subsection{The second universal coefficient}
Writing the normalized logarithmic series as
\[
 \log\left(1+\sum_{j\ge1}C_{d,j}\eps^j\right)
 =\sum_{j\ge1}L_{d,j}\eps^j,
\]
one has $L_{d,1}=C_{d,1}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:b2}
 L_{d,2}=z\left(\frac{d_-}{d_+}\right)^{2/3}\left(\frac1{d_+}-\frac{d_+^2}{27d_-^2}\right)
 =\frac B3\left(\frac1{d+1}-\frac{(d+1)^2}{27(d-1)^2}\right).
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:c2}
 C_{d,2}=\frac{C_{d,1}^2}{2}
       +\frac B3\left(\frac1{d+1}-\frac{(d+1)^2}{27(d-1)^2}\right).
\end{equation}
To derive it, extract $h_2$ from \eqref{tcn:f:carrieridentity} using \eqref{tcn:f:sigma5}. The endpoint contributes $\ell t^2/6$, since $F(t)/(F'(0)t)=1+\ell t^2/6+O(t^3)$. No higher profile or $Z^\Gamma_n$ contributes before order $t^3$. Thus $L_{d,2}=2^{-2/3}(h_2+\ell/6)$, which simplifies to \eqref{tcn:f:b2}.

\emph{[Write phase: the second forms in \eqref{tcn:f:b2} and \eqref{tcn:f:c2} are in the host's $B$ and were checked symbolically against source 104's forms. At $d=2$, $L_{2,2}=0$, as in \eqref{tcn:b:logforward}; at $d=3$, $L_{3,2}=11B/324$, as in \eqref{tcn:d:ternary}. For $d=3,4,5,6,7,8$ the bracket in \eqref{tcn:f:b2} is $11/108$, $118/1215$, $1/12$, $332/4725$, $115/1944$, $22/441$; it has the sign of $27(d-1)^2-(d+1)^3$, so it vanishes at $d=2$, is positive for $3\le d\le21$ (where $L_{d,2}<0$, since $B<0$) and negative for $d\ge22$ (where $L_{d,2}>0$; at $d=21$, $10800>10648$, and at $d=22$, $11907<12167$). Numerically $(C_{d,1},L_{d,2})\approx(0.12053,-0.15002)$, $(-0.14686,-0.16154)$ and $(-0.31838,-0.14869)$ at $d=3,4,5$.]}

The maximal-network expansion has a different second coefficient:
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:maximalfirsttwo}
 \frac{T^{(d)}_{n,n-1}}{(\gamma_d/\Gamma)(n!)^d\Gamma^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^\alpha}
 =1+C_{d,1}n^{-1/3}+\left(C_{d,2}-\frac B3\right)n^{-2/3}+O(n^{-1}).
\end{equation}
Indeed $B[(n-1)^{1/3}-n^{1/3}]=-(B/3)n^{-2/3}+O(n^{-5/3})$. This shift must not be omitted when using word coefficients for network counts. \emph{[Write phase: in Part~I's notation \eqref{tcn:d:netall}, $E_{d,1}=C_{d,1}$ and $E_{d,2}=C_{d,2}-B/3$.]}

For $d=3$, $d_-=2$, $d_+=4$, $\Gamma=32$, $\rho=-1$, $\alpha=-3$, and $B/3=z2^{-2/3}$. In particular,
\begin{align}
 C_{3,1}&=\frac{z^2}{36\,2^{1/3}}=\frac{B^2}{162},\notag\\
 C_{3,2}&=\frac{z^4}{2592\,2^{2/3}}
          +\frac{11z}{108\,2^{2/3}}=\frac{B^4}{52488}+\frac{11B}{324}.\label{tcn:f:ternarycoeffs}
\end{align}
The exact degree-six reconstruction also gives the next ternary logarithmic coefficient. More explicitly,
\begin{align}
 \log\frac{a_n}{\gamma_3(n!)^2 32^n
 \ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{-1}}
 ={}&\frac{z^2}{36\,2^{1/3}}n^{-1/3}
 +\frac{11z}{108\,2^{2/3}}n^{-2/3}\notag\\
 &+\left(\frac{z^3}{243}-\frac3{16}\right)n^{-1}
 +O(n^{-4/3})\qquad(d=3).\label{tcn:f:ternarylog3}
\end{align}
The exact gamma factor contributes $-7/(32n)$ to this expression; omitting it would give the wrong cubic logarithmic coefficient. The corresponding multiplicative third coefficient is
\[
 C_{3,3}=\frac{z^6}{559872}+\frac{43z^3}{7776}-\frac3{16}
 =\frac{B^6}{25509168}+\frac{43B^3}{52488}-\frac3{16}.
\]
Here $a_n$ in \eqref{tcn:f:ternarylog3} denotes the word count at degree $d=3$. The exact reconstruction in the source package checks the recurrence, scalar carrier, local Airy solution and gamma conversion before comparing these coefficients.

\emph{[Write phase: with $B=3z/2^{2/3}$ the three coefficients of \eqref{tcn:f:ternarylog3} are $B^2/162$, $11B/324$ and $4B^3/6561-3/16$, exactly those of Part~I's \eqref{tcn:d:ternary}: two independent derivations, by different gauges, agree. The $B$-forms of $C_{3,2}$ and $C_{3,3}$ are write-phase conversions, checked symbolically; $C_{3,3}$ is new.]}

\subsection{A quantitative consequence of the published ternary proof}\label{tcn:f:sec:ternarytotal}
\begin{proposition}[source 104; conditional on its reading of {\cite{Chang2024}}, see Remark~\ref{tcn:f:rem:reading}]\label{tcn:f:prop:total3rate}
For $d=3$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:totalratio3}
 T^{(3)}_n/T^{(3)}_{n,n-1}=1+O(n^{-1/2}).
\end{equation}
This rate follows directly from the proof of \cite[Lemma 3.24 and Theorem 1.9(ii)]{Chang2024}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The published argument partitions ternary word/network objects into augmentation classes associated with binary objects. In its paired classes, the cardinalities $\mathfrak r(\omega')$ and $\mathfrak s(\omega)$ have the uniform ratio bound
\[
 \frac{\mathfrak r(\omega')}{\mathfrak s(\omega)}\ge \mathfrak C_m,
 \qquad \mathfrak C_m=\prod_{i=2}^m\frac{4i-3}{4i-5}
 =\frac{\Gamma(3/4)}{\Gamma(5/4)}
   \frac{\Gamma(m+1/4)}{\Gamma(m-1/4)}\asymp\sqrt m.
\]
For $n$ leaves the word has exactly $m=n-1$ letters. The source temporarily shifts its word subscript within this paragraph; using $m$ separates the word size from the leaf count. The binary indexing pairs the classes bijectively. Summing $\mathfrak r$ over them gives the ternary maximal count, up to the common permutation factor, whereas summing $\mathfrak s$ gives twice the first-deficit count. That factor two is the marked free-edge encoding in the published construction. Hence, explicitly,
\[
 \frac{T^{(3)}_{n,n-2}}{T^{(3)}_{n,n-1}}
 \le\frac1{2\mathfrak C_{n-1}}=O(n^{-1/2}).
\]
The proof of \cite[Theorem 1.9(ii)]{Chang2024}, by iterating its inequality (15), also gives
\[
 T^{(3)}_{n,n-1-k}\le\frac{T^{(3)}_{n,n-2}}{2^{k-1}k!},
 \qquad 1\le k\le n-1.
\]
Summing the convergent factorial majorant proves \eqref{tcn:f:totalratio3}.
\end{proof}
Combining the all-order maximal theorem with this ratio proves \eqref{tcn:f:total3}: the first maximal correction is $C_{3,1}n^{-1/3}$, and its next error $O(n^{-2/3})$ is smaller than the ratio error $O(n^{-1/2})$. The second word or maximal coefficient in \eqref{tcn:f:maximalfirsttwo} and \eqref{tcn:f:ternarycoeffs} does not transfer with this error bound. The displayed rate is a quantitative extraction from the published proof, not a claim to a separate new combinatorial rate theorem. For $d\ge4$, the published leading ratio suffices for \eqref{tcn:f:totalequivalent}; no unproved quantitative extension is used here.

\begin{remark}[Status of Proposition~\ref{tcn:f:prop:total3rate}; write phase]\label{tcn:f:rem:reading}
The proof above reads two quantitative facts out of the \emph{proofs} of \cite[Lemma 3.24 and Theorem 1.9(ii)]{Chang2024}: the uniform class-ratio bound $\mathfrak C_m$ with $m=n-1$, and the factor two between the summed class sizes and the first-deficit count. Neither is a stated result of that paper (source 104's \textup{\nolinkurl{104-fixdeg-SOURCES.md}} says so), the paper is not shipped, and the reading was not checked against it in writing. The algebra after the two facts is correct: $\mathfrak C_m=\prod_{i=2}^m(i-\frac34)/(i-\frac54)$ telescopes to the displayed Gamma ratio, which is $\asymp\sqrt m$, and $\sum_{k\ge1}1/(2^{k-1}k!)=2(\ee^{1/2}-1)$ is finite. Proposition~\ref{tcn:f:prop:total3rate} and \eqref{tcn:f:total3} are therefore printed as conditional on that reading, and the verification is listed in Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:questions}. If the reading holds, the proposition is the first total-count correction known here for any $d\ge3$ (Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:networks}).
\end{remark}

\section{All order inverses and exact threshold rules}\label{tcn:f:sec:inverse}
\subsection{A common Lambert starting point}
The factorial power must be included in the inverse. Consider a scale
\[
 S(n)=\gamma_\star(n!)^{\mathsf p}\Gamma^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{\mathsf r}(1+o(1)),\qquad \mathsf p>0.
\]
For words use $(\mathsf p,\mathsf r,\gamma_\star)=(d_-,\rho,\gamma_d)$; for maximal networks use $(d,\alpha,\gamma_d/\Gamma)$; for total networks use $(d,\alpha,K_d)$. With $y>0$ large, define
\begin{align}
 x_0(y)&=\frac{y}{\mathsf pW_0(\Gamma^{1/\mathsf p}y/(\mathsf p\ee))},\label{tcn:f:lambert}\\
 L_0(y)&=\mathsf p\log(\Gamma^{1/\mathsf p}x_0(y)),\notag\\
 \hat x_{\mathsf p,\mathsf r}(y)&=x_0(y)-
 \frac{Bx_0(y)^{1/3}+(\mathsf r+\mathsf p/2)\log x_0(y)
                     +(\mathsf p/2)\log(2\pi)}{L_0(y)}.\label{tcn:f:amplitudefree}
\end{align}
Here $W_0$ is the positive real principal branch of Lambert's $W$ function. The point $x_0$ solves
\[
 \mathsf px_0\bigl(\log(\Gamma^{1/\mathsf p}x_0)-1\bigr)=y.
\]
\emph{[Write phase: this is Part~I's seed \eqref{tcn:d:seed}, since $\mathsf px(\log(\Gamma^{1/\mathsf p}x)-1)=\mathsf px\log x+\mathsf ax$ with $\mathsf a=\log\Gamma-\mathsf p$.]}

\begin{proposition}[Amplitude free eventual inverse]\label{tcn:f:prop:amplitudefree}
Even the known two-sided $\Theta$ estimate for any of the three sequences implies
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:inverseweak}
 \hat x_{\mathsf p,\mathsf r}(\log S(n))=n+O(1/\log n).
\end{equation}
Thus nearest-integer rounding eventually recovers the index of an exact sequence value without evaluating the amplitude.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Stirling's formula, the two-sided scale gives $\log S(n)=\Phi(n)+O(1)$, where
\[
 \Phi(x)=\mathsf px\bigl(\log(\Gamma^{1/\mathsf p}x)-1\bigr)
   +Bx^{1/3}+(\mathsf r+\mathsf p/2)\log x+(\mathsf p/2)\log(2\pi).
\]
Write $\Phi=\Phi_0+\varphi_1$. At $x_0$, $\Phi_0(x_0)=y$ and $\Phi_0'(x_0)=L_0$. For $h=-\varphi_1(x_0)/L_0=O(x_0^{1/3}/\log x_0)$,
\[
 \Phi(x_0+h)-y=\varphi_1'(x_0)h+O(h^2/x_0)
                   =O(x_0^{-1/3}/\log x_0).
\]
Since $\Phi'(x)\sim\mathsf p\log x$, the mean value theorem proves \eqref{tcn:f:inverseweak}. The omitted $\log\gamma_\star$ is a bounded constant, so it is consistent with this accuracy.
\end{proof}
The statement is eventual and has no effective numerical cutoff. It does not assert that a floating-point implementation rounds correctly from the first few terms.

\subsection{Arbitrary order word and maximal inverses}
Let $L_{d,j}$ be the logarithmic coefficients defined in Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:coefficients}. With the true canonical amplitude, set
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:FJ}
 \mathcal F_J(x)=d_-\log\Gamma(x+1)+x\log\Gamma+Bx^{1/3}
 +\rho\log x+\log\gamma_d+\sum_{j=1}^JL_{d,j}x^{-j/3},
\end{equation}
where $\Gamma(x+1)$ is Euler's Gamma function and $\log\Gamma$ the logarithm of the base. For sufficiently large $x$, its derivative is positive and asymptotic to $d_-\log x$. If $x_J(y)$ is its unique sufficiently large inverse, Theorem~\ref{tcn:f:thm:main} and the mean value theorem give
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:wordinverse}
 x_J(\log a_n)=n+O\left(\frac{n^{-(J+1)/3}}{\log n}\right).
\end{equation}
The exact shifted maximal model is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:MJ}
 \mathcal M_J(x)=\log\Gamma(x+1)+\mathcal F_J(x-1).
\end{equation}
Its derivative is asymptotic to $d\log x$, and its inverse at $\log T^{(d)}_{n,n-1}$ has the same error order as \eqref{tcn:f:wordinverse}. This shifted model automatically includes every index correction, including the $-B/3$ in \eqref{tcn:f:maximalfirsttwo}.

For either model $\mathcal A_J$, a prescribed finite number of Newton steps suffices asymptotically. Start at \eqref{tcn:f:lambert}, with $\mathsf p=d_-$ for $\mathcal F_J$ or $\mathsf p=d$ for $\mathcal M_J$, and iterate
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:newton}
 x_{k+1}=x_k-\frac{\mathcal A_J(x_k)-y}{\mathcal A_J'(x_k)},\qquad
 m_J=\lceil\log_2(J+4)\rceil-1.
\end{equation}
The $m_J$th iterate already has the inverse error in \eqref{tcn:f:wordinverse}. Indeed, if $x_*$ is the exact model root and $L=\log x_0$, then
\[
 x_*-x_0=O(x_0^{1/3}/L),\qquad
 \mathcal A_J'(x)\asymp L,\qquad
 \mathcal A_J''(x)=O(1/x_0)
\]
in the relevant neighborhood. Taylor's theorem gives
\[
 |x_{k+1}-x_*|\le\frac{C}{x_0L}|x_k-x_*|^2,
 \qquad
 x_k-x_*=O\left(\frac{x_0^{1-2^{k+1}/3}}{L^{2^{k+1}-1}}\right).
\]
The iterates stay in that neighborhood. Since $2^{m_J+1}\ge J+4$, the numerical-model error is at most the asymptotic-model error. All statements here refer to exact mathematical evaluations; uncertified amplitude digits do not provide a certified numerical inverse. \emph{[Write phase: the same quadratic estimate is Part~II's \eqref{tcn:b:newton}.]}

For reference,
\[
 \mathcal F_J'(x)=d_-\Psi(x+1)+\log\Gamma+\frac B3x^{-2/3}
 +\frac\rho x-\sum_{j=1}^J\frac j3L_{d,j}x^{-j/3-1},
\]
where $\Psi$ is the digamma function. If only elementary functions and $W_0$ are desired, replace each $\log\Gamma(x+1)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:stirlinginverse}
 (x+\tfrac12)\log x-x+\tfrac12\log(2\pi)
 +\sum_{m=1}^R\frac{\mathrm B_{2m}}{2m(2m-1)x^{2m-1}},\quad
 R=\max\left(0,\left\lceil\frac{J-2}{6}\right\rceil\right),
\end{equation}
with the Bernoulli numbers $\mathrm B_{2m}$. Its remainder is $O(x^{-2R-1})$ and $2R+1\ge(J+1)/3$. Use the corresponding expression at $x-1$ in the shifted model and differentiate the replacement consistently. The same inverse order and finite Newton count follow.

\subsection{The information available for total counts}
For totals at any fixed degree, the leading model
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:T0}
 \mathcal T_{d,0}(x)=d\log\Gamma(x+1)+x\log\Gamma+Bx^{1/3}
                        +\alpha\log x+\log K_d
\end{equation}
has eventual inverse satisfying
\[
 \mathcal T_{d,0}^{-1}(\log T^{(d)}_n)=n+o(1/\log n).
\]
This uses just the leading equivalent. For $d\ge4$, no power-law remainder for this inverse is inferred from the published ratio limit. For $d=3$, the source-derived refinement gives the stronger model
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:T31}
 \mathcal T_{3,1}(x)=3\log\Gamma(x+1)+x\log32
 +Bx^{1/3}-3\log x+\log(\gamma_3/32)
 +\frac{z^2}{36\,2^{1/3}}x^{-1/3},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:totalinverse3}
 \mathcal T_{3,1}^{-1}(\log T^{(3)}_n)
 =n+O\left(\frac{n^{-1/2}}{\log n}\right).
\end{equation}
Starting from \eqref{tcn:f:lambert} with $\mathsf p=3$, $\Gamma=32$, two Newton steps suffice for this accuracy: their root error is $O(x_0^{-5/3}/\log^7x_0)$, smaller than the model error. This is not an all-order total-count inverse. \emph{[Write phase: \eqref{tcn:f:T31} and \eqref{tcn:f:totalinverse3} inherit the conditional status of Proposition~\ref{tcn:f:prop:total3rate}. At $d=2$ Part~II's \eqref{tcn:b:ht} and \eqref{tcn:b:inverse-total} are stronger.]}

\subsection{Exact floor and ceiling thresholds}
The asymptotic inverse of an exact sequence value and a threshold query are different tasks. A threshold can be arbitrarily close to a count. For words, \eqref{tcn:d:brec} gives, for $n\ge2$,
\[
 a_n\ge b_{n,n}=\binom{d_+n-2}{d_-}a_{n-1}>a_{n-1}.
\]
The initial equality $a_0=a_1=1$ is a finite exception. The maximal sequence is strictly increasing eventually by its exact identity; the total equivalent also gives $T^{(d)}_{n+1}/T^{(d)}_n\sim\Gamma n^d$, hence eventual strict monotonicity. The amplitude-free approximation satisfies $\hat x_{\mathsf p,\mathsf r}'(y)\sim1/(\mathsf p\log x_0)>0$.

Let $S(n)$ be any of these sequences and let $Y$ be a sufficiently large positive threshold. Define
\[
 k_\le(Y)=\max\{n:S(n)\le Y\},\qquad
 j_0=\operatorname{round}(\hat x_{\mathsf p,\mathsf r}(\log Y)).
\]
Since the monotone approximation is $n+o(1)$ at each exact count, $j_0\in\{k_\le(Y),k_\le(Y)+1\}$. Therefore one exact or rigorously certified comparison gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:thresholdfloor}
 k_\le(Y)=\begin{cases}j_0,&S(j_0)\le Y,\\j_0-1,&S(j_0)>Y.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
For the least index $k_\ge(Y)=\min\{n:S(n)\ge Y\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:f:thresholdceiling}
 k_\ge(Y)=\begin{cases}j_0,&S(j_0)\ge Y,\\j_0+1,&S(j_0)<Y.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Equality belongs to the first branch in both formulas. To see the ceiling assertion at equality, note that an exact sufficiently large count rounds to its exact index; strictly between counts the only candidates are the adjacent two. Any deterministic tie rule for nearest-integer rounding works eventually. No unverified floating-point comparison arbitrarily near equality is enough. No effective cutoff or certified numerical precision prescription is supplied here; a practical implementation must certify the comparison and account for the finite initial region. \emph{[Write phase: $k_\ge(Y)$ is Part~II's $\mathcal N(Y)$; the bracket \eqref{tcn:b:threshold} and this one-comparison rule are two forms of the separation condition of Remark~\ref{tcn:d:rem:tai}.]}

\section{Sequence identification and the scope of the literature check}\label{tcn:f:sec:literature}
The $d=2$ word sequence is OEIS A213863 \cite{OEIS}. For $d=3,4$, the original recurrence gives
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{crrrr}
\toprule
$d$ & $a_0$ & $a_1$ & $a_2$ & $a_3$\\
\midrule
2 & 1 & 1 & 7 & 106\\
3 & 1 & 1 & 25 & 2305\\
4 & 1 & 1 & 91 & 51821\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The corresponding maximal-network prefixes, starting at $n=1$, are
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
 d=3:&1,2,150,55320,57939000,132120450000,\ldots,\\
 d=4:&1,2,546,1243704,11351644920,291451508298720,\ldots.
\end{array}
\]
They agree with the maximal entries in \cite[Appendix A, Tables 3--4]{Chang2024}. No OEIS identifier was established for the exact $d=3$ or $d=4$ word or unranked-network sequence in the focused search. This means no entry was located, not that the database contains none. \emph{[Write phase: the $d=3$ row continues Part~I's diagonal in Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:recurrence}; the $d=4$ prefixes and both maximal prefixes were recomputed from \eqref{tcn:d:brec} in writing and agree.]}

A nearby identifier is an important trap: A213864 begins $1,1,19,1075,\ldots$ and is not the $d=3$ sequence. Although it also uses four copies of each letter, it imposes its prefix ordering once a letter has appeared. The $d=3$ condition permits one occurrence before requiring the comparison. Varying multiplicity while keeping the zero-occurrence exception fixed, as in A213275, changes the family. Ranked tree-child counts, including A167484, also count different objects \cite{OEIS213864,OEIS213275,OEIS167484}.

The main baseline is \cite{Chang2024}, whose Theorem 1.8 gives the full Airy $\Theta$ scale, whose Corollary 1.11 gives the ratio limit, and whose Remark 1.12 explicitly distinguishes those facts from a first-order equivalent. Its one-component equivalents concern a different subclass. The positive amplitude above does not rediscover the already published exponent, stretched exponential, or exponential base.

A focused primary-source check through 2 October 2026 located no prior positive-amplitude equivalent or all-order expansion in the fixed-$d\ge3$ maximal-reticulation regime. The relevant newer binary work has different scope: the Young-tableau and Pons--Batle results in \cite{LL2026,LWY2026,YZ2026short} concern exact identities and fixed or bounded reticulation regimes; \cite{YZ2026,FY2026} study sparse binary regimes; and the trident-tableau work \cite{Liu2026} concerns binary networks with exactly one reticulation. These scope distinctions do not constitute an exhaustive priority certificate. The article's mathematical claims rest on the explicit proof above, not on absence of a search result. \emph{[Write phase: in the repository Part~I (source 16) proves the same positive amplitude and all-order expansion for every fixed $d$; it was written independently of source 104 and printed first (Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:provenance}). The sentence is accurate for the literature source 104 inspected.]}

\section{Reproducibility and remaining boundaries}\label{tcn:f:sec:reproducibility}
The accompanying package contains the standalone TeX source, this PDF, build instructions, exact finite certificates and a fail-closed checker. It separates finite algebraic verification from the analytic proof.

The rational checks compare the original recurrence with the rotated one-step recurrence, the two-step evolution, the exact endpoint normalization, the symmetrizer edges and the Gram factor. They also test the repaired contraction at degrees including $d\ge6$, where the naive normalization obstruction matters. The polynomial--Airy checks clear the exact recurrence denominators and check both Airy coordinates, value and derivative gauges, and the modulo-three grading. The endpoint reconstruction uses the normalized Airy differential equation, integrates the scalar ratio, and includes the exact gamma conversion $Z^\Gamma_n$. These tests identify the displayed coefficients and detect algebraic or startup mistakes; they are not machine proofs of the global spectral or all-order analytic estimates.

All verification gates use explicit exceptions, so they remain active under \texttt{python -O}. The release suite is run in ordinary and optimized modes and requires deliberately corrupted inputs to be rejected. The source archive has been rebuilt and replayed from a fresh extraction; every page of the final PDF is visually reviewed after rendering. The shipped provenance identifies the primary sources and relevant versions without redistributing third-party papers or development notes.

\emph{[Write phase: in the repository the checker, runner and build script are \textup{\nolinkurl{code/104-fixdeg-verify_exact.py}}, \textup{\nolinkurl{code/104-fixdeg-run_checks.py}} and \textup{\nolinkurl{code/104-fixdeg-build.sh}}; the inputs, results and provenance are \textup{\nolinkurl{data/104-fixdeg-*}}; the package documentation and review are \textup{\nolinkurl{104-fixdeg-checks-README.md}}, \textup{\nolinkurl{104-fixdeg-checks-RESULTS.md}}, \textup{\nolinkurl{104-fixdeg-SOURCES.md}} and \textup{\nolinkurl{104-fixdeg-qa-REVIEW.md}}. The manuscript, its PDF, the release verifier and the release manifest are not shipped; they survive in the arrival commit. The intake reran the suite on copies of the delivered layout on 5 October 2026. A first run failed after 34 of 50 rejections, during a machine-wide resource shortage, with ``verifier did not produce result JSON''; that case rejects correctly in isolation. A rerun on a fresh copy passed: both baselines (5,655 gates each) and 50 of 50 rejections, in 475 seconds on a loaded laptop. The regenerated result files equal the delivered ones apart from timestamps, durations and the Python version. The release verifier \textup{\nolinkurl{verify_release.py}} fails on Windows (``Unsafe inventory path'': it requires \texttt{str(Path(name)) == name}, which backslashes break); this is a portability defect, not an integrity failure, and the delivered manifest was verified (25 of 25) at placement. The README of this report gives the rerun route.]}

\paragraph{Amplitude digits.}
The convergent representations \eqref{tcn:f:Cproduct}, \eqref{tcn:f:pseries}, and \eqref{tcn:f:gamma} define $\gamma_d$ exactly. Certified decimal enclosures would additionally require effective bounds for the spectral, drift, projection and smoothing tails, together with rigorously controlled finite computations. None is claimed here.

\emph{[Write phase: uncertified diagnostics, computed at the intake (5 October 2026; exact integer recurrence for $n\le900$, $700$, $600$, then least-squares fits of the logarithmic remainder with $C_{d,1}$ fixed by \eqref{tcn:f:c1}; the script is not shipped) give $\gamma_3\approx3.784368$, $\gamma_4\approx3.478351$ and $\gamma_5\approx2.384755$. The fitted second logarithmic coefficients, $-0.15001$, $-0.16151$ and $-0.14867$, agree with \eqref{tcn:f:b2} to four or five digits. Part~I's shipped diagnostics (\textup{\nolinkurl{data/16-dcomb-numerical-diagnostics.json}}) give $3.7843665$ for $d=3$ at $n=6000$, corrected through $n^{-1}$, and, for $d=4$, uncorrected ratios that \emph{increase} from $3.4036$ at $n=500$ to $3.4485$ at $n=6000$, as a negative $C_{4,1}$ requires. None of these digits is certified.]}

\paragraph{Total count corrections.}
The $d=3$ augmentation argument transfers the first coefficient with an $O(n^{-1/2})$ relative remainder. It does not determine the next total coefficient. Sharper deficit-sector asymptotics would be needed for an all-order total expansion; for $d\ge4$ this Part uses only the leading published ratio.

\paragraph{Uniformity and exponentially small terms.}
Every estimate allows dependence on the fixed integer $d$, and on fixed truncation depth where applicable. There is no uniform limit with $d\to\infty$. Formal solvability at every finite depth and a Poincar\'e remainder do not establish a convergent infinite series or a transseries. Separate exponentially small amplitudes and their interaction would require further analysis.

\paragraph{What makes the argument apply at every fixed degree.}
The exact gamma-product normalization, full new-coordinate diagonal, factorwise contraction, form compactness at the half-grid boundary, localization across changing dimension, boundary kernel, strictly positive product comparison, finite polynomial inverse, and signed forced-product bootstrap are all proved here. No unproved analogy with the binary case is needed.

\section{Further questions and research (Part III)}\label{tcn:f:sec:questions}
\emph{Written in the write phase (5 October 2026), under the repository's rule that unproved claims are recorded, not dropped. No claim of source 104 was found to be wrong: every checked constant agrees with Parts~I--II or with an independent computation.}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Verify the ternary reading.} Proposition~\ref{tcn:f:prop:total3rate}, hence \eqref{tcn:f:total3} and \eqref{tcn:f:totalinverse3}, rests on source 104's reading of the proofs of \cite[Lemma 3.24 and Theorem 1.9(ii)]{Chang2024}: the uniform class-ratio bound $\mathfrak C_m\asymp\sqrt m$ with $m=n-1$, and the factor two from the marked free-edge encoding (sketch in the proof above). What is missing is a check of both facts against the paper, or a self-contained proof of $T^{(3)}_{n,n-2}/T^{(3)}_{n,n-1}=O(n^{-1/2})$ from the recurrence. (Source 104.)
\item \emph{Further total-count corrections for $d\ge3$.} The second total coefficient at $d=3$, and any total correction for $d\ge4$, need sharper deficit-sector asymptotics; the published ratio limit gives no rate for $d\ge4$. This is Part~I's question~1 of Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:status}, now re-scoped. At $d=2$ Part~II settles it through the binary identity \eqref{tcn:b:T1}, which has no known analogue for $d\ge3$. (Sources 104 and 16.)
\item \emph{Certified amplitudes.} Enclosures of $\gamma_d$ through \eqref{tcn:f:Cproduct}, \eqref{tcn:f:pseries} need effective constants for the spectral, drift, projection and smoothing tails. The intake values $\gamma_3\approx3.784368$, $\gamma_4\approx3.478351$, $\gamma_5\approx2.384755$ (Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:reproducibility}) are uncertified. Part~I's question~2. (Sources 104 and 16.)
\item \emph{Uniformity in $d$.} All constants depend on $d$; a joint regime with $d$ growing with $n$ is open. The closed forms \eqref{tcn:f:c1}, \eqref{tcn:f:b2} have limits as $d\to\infty$ (for instance $C_{d,1}\to-19z^2/90$, since $B\to3z$), but this proves nothing uniform about the remainders. Part~I's question~3. (Sources 104 and 16.)
\item \emph{Exponentially small terms and convergence.} Neither a transseries nor convergence or divergence of the correction series is established. Part~I's question~4. (Sources 104 and 16.)
\item \emph{Closed forms beyond two coefficients.} \eqref{tcn:f:c1} and \eqref{tcn:f:b2} are universal in $d$; whether every $L_{d,j}$ is a polynomial in $B$ with coefficients rational in $d$ of a recognizable shape, and what the next one is, is not computed. The algorithm of Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:formal} gives each one for fixed $d$. (Write phase.) \textup{[}Added at the independent check: the third coefficient is known exactly at $d=2$, where $L_{2,3}=-1/9$ by \eqref{tcn:b:logforward}, and at $d=3$, where $L_{3,3}=z^3/243-3/16=4B^3/6561-3/16$ by \eqref{tcn:f:ternarylog3}. Fits of exact counts for $n\le1600$, with $L_{d,1}$ and $L_{d,2}$ fixed by \eqref{tcn:f:c1} and \eqref{tcn:f:b2}, give $L_{4,3}\approx-0.3185$ and $L_{5,3}\approx-0.3943$. These are uncertified numerics from the intake's check; a candidate closed form for $L_{d,3}$ should reproduce them.\textup{]}
\item \emph{OEIS.} No accession was established for $d=3,4$ (Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:literature}); none is claimed or submitted. (Source 104.)
\end{enumerate}

\part{The binary word count in the factorial gauge: six logarithmic terms and an identity-free ratio rate (source 102)}\label{tcn:part:w}
\emph{Source 102 (bundle Report 102, archive \textup{\nolinkurl{A213863_Tableaux_and_Tree_Child_Networks_Source}}), printed with its binary-specific material in full and its generic steps as pointers to Part~III at $d=2$; its title was ``Positive Airy amplitudes for Young tableaux and tree child networks: a self contained Jacobi product proof for A213863''. Its abstract reads (symbols renamed as in Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:notation}):} For the Young-tableau and constrained-word sequence $a_n$ recorded as OEIS A213863, we prove the existence of a strictly positive finite amplitude $\gamma_2$ in
\[
 a_n=\gamma_2 n!12^n\ee^{z(3n)^{1/3}}n^{-2/3}\bigl(1+O(n^{-1/3})\bigr),
\]
where $z$ is the largest zero of the Airy function. The exact recurrence is converted into a product of positive contractive operators obtained from finite Jacobi matrices. A Dirichlet Airy limit, quantitative eigenvector drift, and a killed-walk boundary estimate yield a convergent amplitude representation. We then construct compatible polynomial--Airy profiles to arbitrary depth and transfer them to genuine Poincar\'e expansions with the same amplitude. The known exact normalization for maximally reticulated binary tree-child networks and the proved total-to-maximal ratio $\sqrt\ee$ give a first-order equivalent for the total network count. A direct quantitative consequence of published lemmas gives an $O(n^{-2/3})$ ratio error and transfers the first correction to the total count; a full all-order total-network expansion is not claimed. We give exact coefficient algorithms, inverse asymptotics, reproducible finite checks, and precise proof boundaries. No certified decimal value of $\gamma_2$, exponentially small sector, or exhaustive literature-priority claim is made.

\section{Reading Part IV: provenance, credit and notation}\label{tcn:w:sec:reading}
\emph{This section was written when source 102 was added to the report (batch~102, 5 October 2026). In Part~IV, text added in writing is marked ``[Write phase: \dots]''.}

\subsection{Provenance and credit}\label{tcn:w:sec:provenance}
Source 102 is bundle Report~102, archive \textup{\nolinkurl{A213863_Tableaux_and_Tree_Child_Networks_Source.zip}} (543,991 bytes), main file \textup{\nolinkurl{tree_child_amplitude_sources/tree_child_amplitude.tex}} (903 lines, 21-page PDF), dated 2 October 2026. Its title page and its PDF metadata give the author as ``Research prepared for private review''; that wording is the delivery's and is disclosed here, as in the placement commit. It arrived in commit \texttt{60f54ea06} and was placed in this directory by \texttt{6ab1f1979} (batch~102), with the file prefix \textup{\nolinkurl{102-tableaux-}}. It names no repository commit, so it has no pin.

\emph{Credit order.} The archive of source 102 was closed on 1 October 2026 at 22:36 Pacific time (05:36 UTC on 2 October), before the batch-77 archives of sources 16 and 69 reached the repository (\texttt{096ee7b87}, 2 October, 11:05 Pacific). Source 102 thus proves Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:main} (and Theorem~\ref{tcn:d:main} at $d=2$) independently of Parts~I--II. It does not cite source 104 (Part~III), which belongs to the same delivery series, was closed 42 minutes later and shares its architecture. In the repository the theorem was printed first, from sources 16 and 69; source 102 is a further independent route at $d=2$ and is credited as such. Source 104 proves the same theorem for every $d$ with the same architecture in another gauge, so Part~IV is printed after Part~III and points to it where the two arguments coincide at $d=2$.

\subsection{What Part IV proves again and what it adds}\label{tcn:w:sec:map}
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright}p{0.4\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.52\linewidth}@{}}

\subsection{Notation of Part IV}\label{tcn:w:sec:notation}
Part~IV uses Part~II's letters for every notion Part~II names, and for its proof apparatus the letters of Part~III, which play the same roles at $d=2$ in the factorial gauge $3^pp!$ instead of the gamma gauge $3^p(4/3)_p$. Values therefore differ from Part~III's (for example the $n^{-1}$ term of the eigenvalue is $10/3$ here and $3c=2$ there). The renamings of source 102's letters are listed below; no formula changed otherwise. A letter not listed has its Part~III meaning at $d=2$ (Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:notation}) or, failing that, the host's.
