\section{The result and its combinatorial meaning}\label{tcn:w:sec:results}
Let $b_{1,1}=1$, let $b_{n,m}=0$ outside $1\le m\le n$, and for $n\ge2$ let $b_{n,m}=(2n+m-2)\sum_{j\le m}b_{n-1,j}$; this is \eqref{tcn:d:brec} at $d=2$. Set $a_0=1$ and $a_n=\sum_{m=1}^n b_{n,m}$ for $n\ge1$. The first values are
\[
 1,1,7,106,2575,87595,3864040,210455470.
\]
This is OEIS A213863. Its word interpretation uses three copies of each of $n$ letters: in every prefix, whenever a smaller letter has appeared, its count is at least the count of any larger letter. It also counts the three-row Young-tableau family with walls considered in \cite{BW2021}. The recurrence and the network bijection are established in \cite{FYZ2021}.

Let $z=z_1>z_2>\cdots$ be the negative zeros of $\Ai$, the real solution of $\Ai''(x)=x\Ai(x)$ decaying at positive infinity. Thus $z=-2.338107410459767\ldots$; this is a standard Airy constant, distinct from the uncomputed amplitude below.
\begin{theorem}[Positive amplitude; source 102]\label{tcn:w:thm:leading}
There exists $\gamma_2\in(0,\infty)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:main}
 a_n=\gamma_2 n!12^n\ee^{z(3n)^{1/3}}n^{-2/3}
          \left(1+O(n^{-1/3})\right).
\end{equation}
The positive convergent representation in Theorem~\ref{tcn:w:thm:constant} fixes $\gamma_2$ canonically.
\end{theorem}

The already known result for $a_n$ is the two-sided bound \eqref{tcn:b:known-theta} with the same full scale, proved in the network analysis of \cite{FYZ2021} and stated for the identical tableau sequence in \cite[Theorem 4.1]{BW2021}. The existence of a limiting multiplicative constant is the additional assertion here. In particular, the stretched exponential is $z(3n)^{1/3}=Bn^{1/3}$, with no additional factor $3$ before $z$. \emph{[Write phase: Theorem~\ref{tcn:w:thm:leading} is the leading case of Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:main}, and $\gamma_2$ is Part~II's $\gamma$, Part~I's and Part~III's $\gamma_2$: all are the limit of $a_n/(n!12^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{-2/3})$. ``Additional'' refers to the published literature; in the repository Parts~I--II hold the assertion (Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:provenance}).]}

\subsection{Network conventions and the consequence for total counts}
Use the convention of \cite[Definition 1]{FYZ2021}: a rooted, directed acyclic binary phylogenetic network has root indegree zero and outdegree one, $n$ leaves of indegree one and outdegree zero bijectively labelled by $[n]$, tree nodes of indegree one and outdegree two, and reticulation nodes of indegree two and outdegree one. The tree-child condition says that every nonleaf vertex has a child that is a tree node or leaf. Networks are counted up to isomorphism preserving the leaf labels. Write $\TC_{n,k}$ for the count with $k$ reticulations and $\TC_n$ for the total. The maximal possible $k$ is $n-1$.

The exact identity and the later ratio theorem are
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:networkinputs}
 \TC_{n,n-1}=n!a_{n-1},\qquad
 \TC_n\sim\sqrt\ee\,\TC_{n,n-1}.
\end{equation}
The first is \cite[Proposition 2]{FYZ2021}. The second is \cite[Corollary 1.11(i)]{Chang2024}, at $d=2$, under the same root, leaf, and binary-degree convention. Its class $\TC$, rather than its one-component subclass $\mathrm{OTC}$, is used here.
\begin{corollary}[Total binary tree-child networks; source 102]\label{tcn:w:cor:network}
Under these conventions,
\begin{align}
 \TC_n&\sim\frac{\gamma_2\sqrt\ee}{12}(n!)^2 12^n
       \ee^{z(3n)^{1/3}}n^{-5/3}\label{tcn:w:total}\\
 &\sim\frac{\gamma_2\pi\sqrt\ee}{6}\,n^{-2/3}
       \left(\frac{12}{\ee^2}\right)^n n^{2n}\ee^{z(3n)^{1/3}}.\notag
\end{align}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Substitute \eqref{tcn:w:main} at $n-1$ into \eqref{tcn:w:networkinputs}. Use $(n-1)!=n!/n$, $12^{n-1}=12^n/12$, and
$\ee^{z(3(n-1))^{1/3}}/\ee^{z(3n)^{1/3}}=1+O(n^{-2/3})$.
The power changes by the extra $1/n$ to $n^{-5/3}$. Stirling's formula gives the second line.
\end{proof}
\emph{[Write phase: \eqref{tcn:w:total} is the leading term of \eqref{tcn:b:total-forward} and of \eqref{tcn:b:T24}.]} The ratio limit alone would not transfer an error rate. The following quantitative consequence of the same paper's lemmas does so. It is a direct deduction from published estimates, not a separate literature-priority claim.

\begin{proposition}[A quantitative ratio from published lemmas; source 102]\label{tcn:w:prop:ratio-rate}
As $n\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:ratio-rate}
 \frac{\TC_n}{\TC_{n,n-1}}=\sqrt\ee\left(1+O(n^{-2/3})\right).
\end{equation}
Moreover, the reticulation deficit of a uniformly selected binary tree-child network with $n$ leaves has total-variation distance $O(n^{-2/3})$ from $\operatorname{Poisson}(1/2)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For fixed $n\ge2$ put $M=\TC_{n,n-1}$, $\theta_k=\TC_{n,n-1-k}/M$, and $\varpi_k=1/(2^kk!)$, for $0\le k\le n-1$. In the notation of \cite{Chang2024}, $F_{n,r}$ counts networks whose root's child is a free tree node, meaning that neither of that node's children is a reticulation node. Set $\mathfrak f_k=F_{n,n-1-k}/M$. Lemma 3.17 and Remark 3.18 of \cite{Chang2024} give
\[
 \theta_k=2(k+1)\theta_{k+1}+\mathfrak f_k\quad(0\le k\le n-2),\qquad \theta_0=1,\quad \mathfrak f_0=0.
\]
Lemmas 3.19 and 3.20 give, on the full finite range,
\[
 0\le \mathfrak f_k\le\frac{\mathfrak f_1}{2^{k-1}(k-1)!}\quad(1\le k\le n-1),\qquad
 \mathfrak f_1=O(n^{-2/3}).
\]
Since $\varpi_k=2(k+1)\varpi_{k+1}$, induction yields
\[
 0\le\frac{\varpi_k-\theta_k}{\varpi_k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{\mathfrak f_j}{\varpi_j}
        \le2\mathfrak f_1\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}j=\mathfrak f_1k(k-1).
\]
Write $\sum_{k\ge0}\varpi_k=\sqrt\ee$ and $\mathcal R_{n-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\theta_k=\TC_n/M$.
The factorial second moment satisfies $\sum_{k\ge0}k(k-1)\varpi_k=\sqrt\ee/4$. Therefore the exact finite bound is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:finite-deficit}
 0\le \sqrt\ee-\mathcal R_{n-1}\le\frac{\sqrt\ee}4\mathfrak f_1+\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\frac1{2^kk!}.
\end{equation}
The tail is at most $[2^nn!(1-1/(2(n+1)))]^{-1}$, hence smaller than every negative power of $n$, proving \eqref{tcn:w:ratio-rate}. Extend $\theta_k=0$ for $k\ge n$. The actual deficit law is $\theta_k/\mathcal R_{n-1}$ and the Poisson law is $\varpi_k/\sqrt\ee$. Since $0\le \theta_k\le \varpi_k$,
\[
 \frac12\sum_{k\ge0}\left|\frac{\theta_k}{\mathcal R_{n-1}}-\frac{\varpi_k}{\sqrt\ee}\right|
 \le\frac12\left(\frac{\sqrt\ee-\mathcal R_{n-1}}{\sqrt\ee}+\frac{\sqrt\ee-\mathcal R_{n-1}}{\sqrt\ee}\right)
 =\frac{\sqrt\ee-\mathcal R_{n-1}}{\sqrt\ee}=O(n^{-2/3}).
\]
This proves the total-variation assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Relation to Part~II; write phase]\label{tcn:w:rem:tv}
Proposition~\ref{tcn:w:prop:ratio-rate} is weaker than Part~II, which has every finite order of $\mathcal R_n$ (Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:transfer}) and the exact eventual identity $d_{\mathrm{TV}}=\tfrac32(1/\mathcal R_n-\ee^{-1/2})$ with its expansion (Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:tv}). It is kept as a second route because it uses only Lemmas 3.17--3.20 and Remark 3.18 of \cite{Chang2024}, not the Pons--Batle identity \eqref{tcn:b:T1} proved in \cite{LL2026}, on which Part~II's transfer rests. The two are consistent. Part~II's identity can be written $d_{\mathrm{TV}}=\frac3{2\mathcal R_n}\cdot\frac{\sqrt\ee-\mathcal R_n}{\sqrt\ee}$; since $\mathcal R_n\to\sqrt\ee>3/2$, the prefactor tends to $3/(2\sqrt\ee)=0.9098\ldots<1$, so the exact value lies below source 102's bound $(\sqrt\ee-\mathcal R_n)/\sqrt\ee$ for all large $n$, and both are of exact order $n^{-2/3}$ because $\mathcal R_n=\sqrt\ee(1+\tfrac B{72}n^{-2/3}+O(n^{-1}))$ with $B\ne0$ (proof of Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:tv}; equivalently, the leading coefficient $|B|/(48\sqrt\ee)\neq0$ of \eqref{tcn:b:D8}) \textup{[}corrected at the independent check; see the end of Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:questions}\textup{]}. Source 102 counts $n$ leaves and Part~II $n+1$; the shift does not affect these orders. Source 102 cites \cite{LL2026} but does not use it.
\end{remark}

In particular \eqref{tcn:w:total} has relative error $O(n^{-1/3})$ by Theorem~\ref{tcn:w:thm:leading}. Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:coefficients} sharpens it by one explicit correction. The all-order theorem below applies to $a_n$ and, by exact shifting, to $\TC_{n,n-1}$. The quantitative ratio bound alone does not yield every correction for $\TC_n$.

\subsection{Position relative to the inspected literature}\label{tcn:w:sec:position}
The final discussion of \cite{FYZ2021} asks for a first-order equivalent and identifies two obstacles: the amplitude of $a_n$, and the total/maximal ratio. The latter was resolved by \cite{Chang2024}, whose Remark 1.12 still points to the former. The result here supplies the amplitude input. \emph{[Write phase: so do Parts~I--II and Part~III, independently; in the repository Part~II, from source 69, was printed first and goes further (Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:provenance}).]}
The September 2026 version of \cite{LL2026} proves an enumerative identity, while its asymptotics fix the number of reticulations. The 2026 works \cite{LWY2026,YZ2026,FY2026} concern bounded or sparse reticulation regimes, not $k=n-1$. The September paper \cite{YZ2026short} supplies a short proof of the exact Pons--Batle enumerative identity and recurrence, rather than a diagonal asymptotic theorem. The April 2026 version of \cite{EFLW2026} discusses tableau and phylogenetic applications in its Section 10.1. These inspected sources locate the particular gap addressed here. They do not constitute an exhaustive worldwide priority certificate. Formal Airy expansions and numerical extrapolation are established techniques; the analytic convergence and transfer arguments, rather than the ansatz alone, carry the theorem.

\section{The exact finite Jacobi product in the factorial gauge}\label{tcn:w:sec:jacobi}
\subsection{Safe startup and rotated coordinates}
The prefix sum in the recurrence is constant above the preceding diagonal, not zero. Subtracting neighboring prefix identities gives, away from the separately initialized first entry,
\[
 b_{n,m}=(2n+m-2)b_{n-1,m}
       +\frac{2n+m-2}{2n+m-3}b_{n,m-1}.
\]
Put $\widetilde b(p,q)=b_{p+1,q+1}$ for $0\le q\le p$, and set it to zero elsewhere. Then $\widetilde b(0,0)=1$ and, at noninitial allowed indices,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:B}
 \widetilde b(p,q)=(2p+q+1)\widetilde b(p-1,q)
       +\frac{2p+q+1}{2p+q}\widetilde b(p,q-1).
\end{equation}
The only zero denominator would be at the excluded initial entry. The zero term with $q=-1$ causes no difficulty at any other index. Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:diagonal}
 a_n=\frac{\widetilde b(n,n)}{3n+1},\qquad n\ge0.
\end{equation}
\emph{[Write phase: \eqref{tcn:w:B} and \eqref{tcn:w:diagonal} are \eqref{tcn:f:B} and \eqref{tcn:f:endpointexact} at $d=2$, where $\mathcal W(p,q)=2p+q+1$ and $\mathcal W(n,n)=3n+1$.]}
For integers $N\ge h\ge0$ of the same parity, define
\[
 \mathcal Y(N,h)=\frac{\widetilde b((N+h)/2,(N-h)/2)}{3^{(N+h)/2}((N+h)/2)!}.
\]
Outside this wedge or off parity let $\mathcal Y=0$, and let $\mathcal Y(0,0)=1$. Equation~\eqref{tcn:w:B} yields
\begin{align}
 \mathcal Y(N,h)&=\omega^-_N(h)\mathcal Y(N-1,h-1)+\omega^+_N(h)\mathcal Y(N-1,h+1),\label{tcn:w:walk}\\
 \omega^-_N(h)&=\frac{3N+h+2}{3(N+h)},&
 \omega^+_N(h)&=\frac{3N+h+2}{3N+h}.\notag
\end{align}
This is evaluated only at noninitial allowed indices. Its lower ghost coordinate is $\mathcal Y(N,-1)=0$. \emph{[Write phase: Part~III divides by $G_\Gamma(p)=3^p(4/3)_p$ at $d=2$ instead of $3^pp!$; the two gauges differ by $(4/3)_p/p!\sim p^{1/3}/\Gamma(4/3)$, which accounts for the different powers and $n^{-1}$ terms below.]}

Define $e_n(k)=\mathcal Y(2n,2k)$ for $0\le k\le n$, and zero-extend vectors to $\ell^2(\N_0)$. Eliminating the odd step gives $e_0=\delta_0$ and $e_n=\mathsf M_ne_{n-1}$, where
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:A}
 (\mathsf M_nu)(k)=\mathsf L_n(k)u(k-1)+\mathsf D_n(k)u(k)+\mathsf U_n(k)u(k+1)
\end{equation}
with
\begin{align}
 \mathsf L_n(k)&=\frac{(3n+k+1)(3n+k-1)}{9(n+k)(n+k-1)},&&1\le k\le n,\notag\\
 \mathsf U_n(k)&=\frac{3n+k+1}{3n+k-1},&&0\le k<n,\label{tcn:w:coeff}\\
 \mathsf D_n(k)&=\frac{(3n+k+1)(6n+2k-3)}{3(n+k)(3n+k-2)},&&1\le k\le n,\notag\\
 \mathsf D_n(0)&=\frac{3n+1}{3n}.&&\notag
\end{align}
Missing off-diagonal terms at $0$ and $n$ are omitted. We deliberately keep the full bulk diagonal at $k=n$: its extra down-up contribution multiplies $e_{n-1}(n)=0$, so the actual evolution is unchanged. This choice removes an exceptional old terminal diagonal from the later drift estimate.

\subsection{Symmetrization and positivity}
Let $(x)_k=x(x+1)\cdots(x+k-1)$, with $(x)_0=1$. Define $\Sigma_n(0)=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:S}
 \Sigma_n(k)^2=\frac{(3n-1)_k(3n)_k(3n+2)_k}
 {9^k(n)_k(n+1)_k(3n+1)_k},\qquad 1\le k\le n.
\end{equation}
Also $\Sigma_0(0)=1$. Since $\Sigma_n(k)/\Sigma_n(k-1)=\sqrt{\mathsf L_n(k)/\mathsf U_n(k-1)}$, the matrix $J_n=\Sigma_n^{-1}\mathsf M_n\Sigma_n$ is symmetric, with diagonal $\mathsf D_n$ and off-diagonal
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:beta}
 \mathsf b_n(k)=\sqrt{\frac{(3n+k+1)(3n+k-1)(3n+k)}
 {9(n+k)(n+k-1)(3n+k-2)}},\qquad1\le k\le n.
\end{equation}
It factors as $J_n=\mathsf K_n\mathsf K_n^{\mathsf T}$, where $\mathsf K_n$ is the square lower bidiagonal matrix of order $n+1$ whose nonzero entries are positive and obey
\begin{align}
 \mathsf K_n(k,k)^2&=\frac{3n+k+1}{3(n+k)},&&0\le k\le n,\label{tcn:w:factor}\\
 \mathsf K_n(k,k-1)^2&=\frac{(3n+k+1)(3n+k-1)}{3(n+k)(3n+k-2)},&&1\le k\le n.\notag
\end{align}
The adjacent squared products give $\mathsf b_n(k)^2$, and the diagonal sums give precisely \eqref{tcn:w:coeff}, including $\mathsf D_n(0)$. Thus $J_n$ is positive definite on its finite support and positive semidefinite after zero extension. Let $\lambda_n$ be its largest eigenvalue and $\psi_n$ its strictly positive unit eigenvector. Set $\psi_0=\delta_0$.

\subsection{A contractive change of gauge between times}
For $k<n$, put $\mathsf R_n(k)=\Sigma_{n-1}(k)/\Sigma_n(k)$; put $\mathsf R_n(k)=0$ for $k\ge n$, with $\mathsf R_1(0)=1$.
\begin{lemma}[source 102]\label{tcn:w:lem:R}
For every $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:Rbound}
 0\le \mathsf R_n\le I,\qquad 0\le1-\mathsf R_n(k)\le Ck^2/n^2\quad(0\le k\le n).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $\varsigma_n(j)=\Sigma_n(j)/\Sigma_n(j-1)$. Its square factors as $\mathfrak f_1\mathfrak f_2$, where
\[
 \mathfrak f_1=\frac{3n+j-1}{3(n+j-1)},\qquad
 \mathfrak f_2=\frac{(3n+j-2)(3n+j+1)}{3(n+j)(3n+j)}.
\]
Both lie in $(0,1]$ and increase with real $n\ge1$. For $\mathfrak f_1$ this follows from
$\mathfrak f_1=1-2(j-1)/(3(n+j-1))$. For $\mathfrak f_2$, set $\mathfrak z=3n+j$, $\mathfrak n=n+j$; then
\[
 \mathfrak f_2=\frac{\mathfrak z-1-2/\mathfrak z}{3\mathfrak n},\qquad
 \partial_n\mathfrak f_2=\frac{2j+1+6\mathfrak n/\mathfrak z^2+2/\mathfrak z}{3\mathfrak n^2}>0.
\]
Thus $\mathsf R_n(k)=\prod_{j=1}^k \varsigma_{n-1}(j)/\varsigma_n(j)\le1$ on the common support.
For $n-1\le t\le n$ and $j\le n-1$, the factors are uniformly bounded below and
$|\partial_t\log \varsigma_t(j)|\le C(j+1)/n^2$. Integration and summation give
$0\le-\log \mathsf R_n(k)\le Ck(k+1)/n^2$. For integral $k\ge1$ this implies \eqref{tcn:w:Rbound}; $k=0$ is exact, and $k=n$ is covered by increasing $C$.
\end{proof}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:T}
 P_n=\prod_{j=1}^n\lambda_j,\quad P_0=1,\qquad
 u_n=P_n^{-1}\Sigma_n^{-1}e_n,\qquad
 \mathsf T_n=(J_n/\lambda_n)\mathsf R_n.
\end{equation}
Then $u_n=\mathsf T_nu_{n-1}$ exactly. Positive semidefiniteness makes $J_n/\lambda_n$ a Hilbert-space contraction; Lemma~\ref{tcn:w:lem:R} does the same for $\mathsf R_n$. All matrices are entrywise nonnegative. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:contract}
 \norm{\mathsf T_n}_{2\to2}\le1,\qquad\norm{u_n}_2\le1.
\end{equation}

\section{A quantitative frozen Airy limit, localization and smoothing}\label{tcn:w:sec:spectral}
We prove all the spectral estimates needed in the product argument. Let $\lambda_n^{(2)}$ be the second largest eigenvalue, with the spectrum sorted in decreasing order.
\begin{proposition}[source 102]\label{tcn:w:prop:spectral}
As $n\to\infty$,
\begin{align}
 \lambda_n&=4+4\cdot3^{-2/3}z\,n^{-2/3}+\frac{10}{3}n^{-1}
                 +O(n^{-4/3}),\label{tcn:w:lambda}\\
 0\le\lambda_n^{(2)}/\lambda_n&\le1-cn^{-2/3},\label{tcn:w:gap}\\
 \norm{k^p\psi_n}_2&\le C_pn^{p/3}\quad(p\ge0\text{ fixed}),\label{tcn:w:moments}\\
 \norm{\psi_n-\psi_{n-1}}_2&\le C/n,\label{tcn:w:drift}\\
 \psi_n(0)&=\sqrt{2/3}\,n^{-1/2}\bigl(1+O(n^{-2/3})\bigr).\label{tcn:w:endpointphi}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\emph{[Write phase: in \eqref{tcn:w:gap} $c$ is a positive constant, not $2/3$. The terms $4\cdot3^{-2/3}z=c_A^2z$ and $\sqrt{2/3}=\sqrt c$ agree with Part~III's \eqref{tcn:f:lambda} and \eqref{tcn:f:perronendpoint} at $d=2$; the $n^{-1}$ term is $10/3$ here and $3c=2$ there, because the gauges differ.]}

\subsection{Form convergence and the spectral gap}
Put $\eps=n^{-1/3}$ and $x_k=\eps(k+1/2)$. The continuum operator is
\[
 \mathcal H_A=-\frac{d^2}{dx^2}+\frac83x
 \quad\text{on }L^2(0,\infty),\qquad f(0)=0.
\]
Write $c_A=2\cdot3^{-1/3}$. Its eigenvalues are $-c_A^2z_j$, and its positive unit ground state is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:airyf}
 f_A(x)=\frac{\sqrt{c_A}\,\Ai(c_Ax+z)}{\Ai'(z)}.
\end{equation}
The Airy equation and the integral of $[\Ai'(x)^2-x\Ai(x)^2]'=-\Ai(x)^2$ give $\int_0^\infty f_A^2=1$ and $f_A'(0)=c_A^{3/2}=\sqrt{8/3}$.

For this paragraph set $\mathsf b_n(0)=\mathsf b_n(n+1)=0$ and
$\mathsf V_n(k)=4-\mathsf D_n(k)-\mathsf b_n(k)-\mathsf b_n(k+1)$. The exact quadratic form is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:form}
 \ip{u}{(4I-J_n)u}
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathsf b_n(k+1)|u(k+1)-u(k)|^2
    +\sum_{k=0}^n\mathsf V_n(k)|u(k)|^2.
\end{equation}
For $1\le k\le n$, let $\vartheta_n(k)=(3n+k)/(3(n+k))$. Factoring the explicit rational coefficients gives, uniformly on existing bulk edges,
\[
 \mathsf D_n(k)=2\vartheta_n(k)+O(n^{-1}),\quad
 \mathsf b_n(k)=\vartheta_n(k)+O(n^{-1}),\quad
 \mathsf b_n(k+1)=\vartheta_n(k)+O(n^{-1})\quad(k<n).
\]
For example, with $\mathfrak z=3n+k$, $\mathfrak n=n+k$,
\[
 \mathsf D_n(k)=\frac{\mathfrak z}{3\mathfrak n}\frac{(1+1/\mathfrak z)(2-3/\mathfrak z)}{1-2/\mathfrak z},\qquad
 \mathsf b_n(k)^2=\left(\frac{\mathfrak z}{3\mathfrak n}\right)^2
        \frac{1-1/\mathfrak z^2}{(1-1/\mathfrak n)(1-2/\mathfrak z)}.
\]
All denominators are bounded away from zero for large $n$. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:potential}
 \mathsf V_n(k)\ge\frac{4k}{3n}-\frac Cn\quad(k\ge1),\qquad \mathsf V_n(0)=2+O(n^{-1}),
\end{equation}
and existing $\mathsf b_n(k)$ are bounded above and below by positive constants. The omitted edge at $n$ only increases the potential. On fixed scaled compact intervals away from zero,
\[
 \mathsf b_n(k)\longrightarrow1,\qquad \mathsf V_n(k)/\eps^2\longrightarrow\tfrac83x_k
\]
uniformly. Adding $C/n$ to the quadratic form makes its potential nonnegative; the scaled added energy is $O(\eps)$ and disappears in the limit.

Here are details of the compactness and boundary argument. A unit vector with form at most $C\eps^2$ satisfies
\[
 \sum k|u(k)|^2\le C/\eps,\qquad |u(0)|^2\le C\eps^2,\qquad
 \sum|u(k+1)-u(k)|^2\le C\eps^2.
\]
Interpolate $u(k)/\sqrt\eps$ linearly at $x_k$, from zero at $x=0$ to the first node, and from the last node to zero one mesh interval later; set the function to zero thereafter. The first half-cell has derivative energy $2|u(0)|^2/\eps^2$, supplied by $\mathsf V_n(0)\to2$. The other cells give a bounded $H^1$ seminorm on every fixed compact interval. The moving final ramp eventually lies outside each such interval. Its mass, and all mass beyond $x=R$, are $O(1/R)$ by the first-moment estimate.

Local Rellich compactness and this tail bound yield strong global $L^2$ compactness. Locally the difference from the piecewise-constant embedding is bounded by $C\eps$ times the $H^1$ seminorm, while both embeddings have small tails. Norms and inner products, hence orthogonality, are preserved in the limit. Weak lower semicontinuity gives the gradient liminf including the boundary half-cell. For the potential liminf, first restrict away from zero to a fixed compact interval, use uniform convergence, then enlarge the interval. The negative $O(n^{-1})$ part vanishes after scaling. Smooth compactly supported Dirichlet trial functions sampled on the grid supply matching upper bounds. The min--max principle on each fixed-dimensional trial or low-energy subspace now gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:minmax}
 (4-\lambda_n^{(j)})/\eps^2\longrightarrow -c_A^2z_j
 \quad\text{for every fixed }j.
\end{equation}
This identifies the top eigenvalue and proves \eqref{tcn:w:gap}; positive semidefiniteness controls the opposite end of the spectrum. \emph{[Write phase: this is the argument of Part~III, Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:spectral}, at $d=2$, written out in more detail; compare Remark~\ref{tcn:d:rem:compact}.]}

\subsection{A global quasimode and the coefficient ten thirds}
Sample $\sqrt\eps f_A(x_k)$ for $0\le k\le n$ and normalize it to a unit vector $\psi^A_n$. Midpoint quadrature and Airy decay give the pre-normalization norm $1+O(\eps^2)$. In the bulk, direct expansion gives
\begin{align}
 \mathsf D_n(k)&=2-\tfrac43x_k\eps^2+\tfrac53\eps^3
                  +O\bigl(\eps^4(1+x_k^2)\bigr),\notag\\
 \mathsf b_n(k)&=1-\tfrac23x_k\eps^2+\tfrac76\eps^3
                  +O\bigl(\eps^4(1+x_k^2)\bigr),\label{tcn:w:expansions}\\
 \mathsf b_n(k+1)&=1-\tfrac23x_k\eps^2+\tfrac12\eps^3
                  +O\bigl(\eps^4(1+x_k^2)\bigr).\notag
\end{align}
The remainders are uniform for $k\le n/2$, since the rescaled denominators stay separated from zero. Also $|\mathsf b_n(k+1)-\mathsf b_n(k)|\le C/n$ on the entire bulk. Pair the Taylor expansions of $f_A(x+\eps)$ and $f_A(x-\eps)$. The operator then acts as
\[
 J_nf_A=4f_A+\eps^2(f_A''-\tfrac83xf_A)+\tfrac{10}{3}\eps^3f_A
                   +O_{\ell^2}(\eps^4).
\]
To justify the global norm, the unsampled remainder is bounded by $C\eps^4$ times a fixed polynomial in $1+x$ times the maximum of $|f_A^{(j)}(y)|$, $j\le4$, $|y-x|\le\eps$, $y\ge0$. The odd derivative terms carry the coefficient differences $\eps(\mathsf b_n(k+1)-\mathsf b_n(k))$ and $\eps^3(\mathsf b_n(k+1)-\mathsf b_n(k))/6$, and fit this bound. The Airy equation and decay bound the envelope by $C(1+x)^{M'}\ee^{-c'x^{3/2}}$, whose squared sampled norm $\eps\sum$ is uniformly bounded. The region $k>n/2$ contributes exponentially little in $n$.

At zero, $\mathsf D_n(0)=1+\eps^3/3$ and $\mathsf b_n(1)=1+\eps^3/6+O(\eps^6)$. Since
$f_A(0)=f_A''(0)=0$ and $f_A'''(0)=c_A^2zf_A'(0)$, the cubic term in
$f_A(3\eps/2)-3f_A(\eps/2)$ cancels $c_A^2z\eps^2f_A(\eps/2)$. The remaining unsampled boundary residual is $O(\eps^4)$, hence the sampled one is $O(\eps^{9/2})$. The terminal diagonal affects only an exponentially small term. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:residual}
 \norm{\bigl(J_n-[4+c_A^2z\eps^2+\tfrac{10}{3}\eps^3]\bigr)\psi^A_n}_2
                  \le C\eps^4.
\end{equation}
For a self-adjoint matrix, expansion in an orthonormal eigenbasis puts an eigenvalue within the residual norm of the proposed value. The gap from \eqref{tcn:w:minmax} identifies it as $\lambda_n$ and proves \eqref{tcn:w:lambda}. The same expansion, projected off the Perron vector, gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:qclose}
 \norm{\psi_n-\psi^A_n}_2\le C\eps^2.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Uniform moments and the moving upper edge}
Let $J_\infty$ have diagonal $1$ at zero, diagonal $2$ elsewhere, and off-diagonals $1$. The coefficient formulas give the entrywise comparison
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:domination}
 0\le J_n\le(1+C/n)J_\infty.
\end{equation}
The factor $1+C/n$ is necessary: some downward weights exceed one and $\mathsf D_n(0)=1+1/(3n)>1$. For the nonnegative unit Perron vector,
\[
 \ip{\psi_n}{(4I-J_\infty)\psi_n}
 \le4-\lambda_n/(1+C/n)\le C\eps^2.
\]
The left side is $\sum_{k\ge0}|\psi_n(k+1)-\psi_n(k)|^2+2\psi_n(0)^2$.
Telescoping $u(k)^2$ from infinity and applying Cauchy--Schwarz bounds $\norm{u}_\infty^2$ by $2\norm{u}_2\norm{\nabla u}_2$, with $\nabla$ the forward difference. Thus
$\norm{\psi_n}_\infty\le C\sqrt\eps$.

Let $k_A=\lceil A_0/\eps\rceil$, with fixed $A_0$ sufficiently large, and choose a small fixed $c_0>0$. On $k>k_A$ take $w(k)=\ee^{-c_0\eps(k-k_A)}$. For this estimate only, continue the explicit off-diagonal formula to the fictitious positive edge $\mathsf b_n(n+1)$. The full row sum is at most $4-c'k/n+C/n$, while adjacent coefficients differ by $O(n^{-1})$. Replacing neighboring values by $\ee^{\pm c_0\eps}w(k)$ changes the full row sum by $O(\eps^2)$: the symmetric part is $O(\eps^2)$ and the antisymmetric part is $O(\eps/n)$. Choose $A_0$ large enough and use \eqref{tcn:w:lambda}; then $(J_nw)(k)<\lambda_nw(k)$ for $k>k_A$. Omitting the fictitious positive upper-edge contribution at $k=n$ only improves this inequality.

The finite maximum principle applied to $\psi_n(k)/w(k)$, with its value at $k_A$ as boundary, gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:tail}
 \psi_n(k)\le C\sqrt\eps\,\ee^{-c_0\eps(k-k_A)},\qquad k\ge k_A.
\end{equation}
Indeed a strictly larger interior maximum would contradict the positive supersolution inequality. Together with the supremum bound below $k_A$, this proves every moment estimate \eqref{tcn:w:moments}. In particular the mass beyond $n/2$ is exponentially small in $n^{2/3}$.

On common coordinates, differentiating the explicit rational factors yields
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:coefdrift}
 |\mathsf D_n(k)-\mathsf D_{n-1}(k)|+|\mathsf b_n(k)-\mathsf b_{n-1}(k)|
                  \le C(k+1)/n^2.
\end{equation}
For instance, $\partial_n\vartheta_n(k)=2k/(3(n+k)^2)$, while derivatives of its correction factors are $O(n^{-2})$. There is no old exceptional terminal diagonal, by our full-bulk choice. The new edge $\mathsf b_n(n)$ is order one, but acts on $\psi_{n-1}(n-1)$, exponentially small by \eqref{tcn:w:tail}; the new diagonal acts on the zero extension. Hence
\[
 \norm{(J_n-J_{n-1})\psi_{n-1}}_2
 \le Cn^{-2}\norm{(k+1)\psi_{n-1}}_2+O(\ee^{-cn^{2/3}})
 \le Cn^{-5/3}.
\]
The continuum spectral limit separates $\lambda_{n-1}$ from every non-Perron eigenvalue of $J_n$ by $cn^{-2/3}$. Orthogonal projection of this residual proves \eqref{tcn:w:drift}, with the positive signs of both eigenvectors fixing the phase. Lemma~\ref{tcn:w:lem:R} and the second moment estimate also give
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:Rsmall}
 \norm{(I-\mathsf R_n)\psi_n}_2+\norm{(I-\mathsf R_n)\psi_{n-1}}_2\le Cn^{-4/3}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The killed half-line kernel}
Put $\mathcal K=J_\infty/4$. The functions $\sin((k+1/2)\theta)$ diagonalize $J_\infty$, with eigenvalue $2+2\cos\theta$ and squared integral $\pi/2$ on $(0,\pi)$. Therefore
\[
 \mathcal K^m(0,0)=\frac2\pi\int_0^\pi
       \cos^{2m}(\theta/2)\sin^2(\theta/2)\,d\theta
       \le C(m+1)^{-3/2}.
\]
The bound follows by splitting away from zero and using Gaussian decay near zero, where the weight is $O(\theta^2)$. By symmetry, $\norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}\mathcal K^l}_2^2=\mathcal K^{2l}(0,0)$ and $\norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}\mathcal K^l}_2\le C(l+1)^{-3/4}$.
For $0\le l\le n^{2/3}$, positivity, \eqref{tcn:w:domination}, and $\mathsf R_j\le I$ imply
\[
 0\le \mathsf T_n\cdots \mathsf T_{n-l+1}
 \le\left(\prod_{j=n-l+1}^n\frac{4(1+C/j)}{\lambda_j}\right)\mathcal K^l\le C'\mathcal K^l.
\]
The logarithm of the scalar product is $O(ln^{-2/3})=O(1)$ by \eqref{tcn:w:lambda}. This entrywise argument does not assume commutation of the varying matrices. We conclude
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:smoothing}
 \norm{\delta_0^{\mathsf T}\mathsf T_n\cdots \mathsf T_{n-l+1}}_2
             \le C(l+1)^{-3/4},\qquad 0\le l\le n^{2/3}.
\end{equation}
The identical proof bounds $\delta_0^{\mathsf T}(J_n/\lambda_n)^l$ on that window. \emph{[Write phase: this is \eqref{tcn:f:kernel}--\eqref{tcn:f:smoothing} of Part~III at $d=2$, with the sine basis written out.]}

Let $\mathsf z=\psi_n-\psi^A_n$. Equations~\eqref{tcn:w:lambda} and \eqref{tcn:w:residual} give
$\mathsf z=(J_n/\lambda_n)\mathsf z+\varrho$, with $\norm{\varrho}_2\le C\eps^4$ and $\norm{\mathsf z}_2\le C\eps^2$.
Iterate $\Delta=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ times and use the fixed-matrix version of \eqref{tcn:w:smoothing}:
\[
 |\mathsf z(0)|\le C(\Delta+1)^{-3/4}\eps^2
     +C\eps^4\sum_{l=0}^{\Delta-1}(l+1)^{-3/4}=O(\eps^{7/2}).
\]
Since $\psi^A_n(0)=\tfrac12f_A'(0)\eps^{3/2}(1+O(\eps^2))$, this proves \eqref{tcn:w:endpointphi} and completes Proposition~\ref{tcn:w:prop:spectral}.

\section{Convergence and strict positivity of the amplitude}\label{tcn:w:sec:amplitude}
Let
\[
 p_n=\ip{\psi_n}{u_n},\qquad
 p_n^\perp=\norm{(I-\psi_n\psi_n^{\mathsf T})u_n}_2.
\]
Project the exact recurrence $u_n=(J_n/\lambda_n)\mathsf R_nu_{n-1}$ onto the complement of $\psi_n$. Use the spectral gap on that complement, contractivity of $\mathsf R_n$, the $O(n^{-1})$ drift, and \eqref{tcn:w:Rsmall}. Since $\norm{u_n}_2\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:perp}
 p_n^\perp\le(1-cn^{-2/3})p_{n-1}^\perp+C/n.
\end{equation}
A sufficiently large multiple of $n^{-1/3}$ is a supersolution for large $n$; absorb the finite initial segment into its constant. Thus $p_n^\perp=O(n^{-1/3})$.

Because $(J_n/\lambda_n)\psi_n=\psi_n$, the scalar increment is exactly
\begin{align}
 p_n-p_{n-1}
 &=p_{n-1}\bigl[\ip{\psi_n}{\mathsf R_n\psi_{n-1}}-1\bigr]\notag\\
 &\quad+\ip{\mathsf R_n\psi_n-\psi_{n-1}}
 {(I-\psi_{n-1}\psi_{n-1}^{\mathsf T})u_{n-1}}.\label{tcn:w:increment}
\end{align}
Without $\mathsf R_n$, the first bracket is $-\norm{\psi_n-\psi_{n-1}}_2^2/2=O(n^{-2})$; its $\mathsf R_n$ correction is $O(n^{-4/3})$. The second line is $O(n^{-1}p_{n-1}^\perp)=O(n^{-4/3})$. Consequently the scalar increments are absolutely summable. There is $p_\infty\ge0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:l2limit}
 |p_n-p_\infty|+\norm{u_n-p_\infty\psi_n}_2\le Cn^{-1/3}.
\end{equation}

A separate comparison proves strict positivity. Restart the normalized evolution at a sufficiently large $N$ with initial vector $\psi_N$. Its norm is one and its perpendicular component initially vanishes. The same supersolution and increment bounds have constants independent of $N$ once $N$ is above a fixed threshold. Summing the increments of this restarted product shows that its limiting Perron coefficient $p_\infty'$ satisfies $|p_\infty'-1|\le CN^{-1/3}$. Choose $N$ so this is less than $1/2$.
Every coordinate of the actual $u_N$ and $\psi_N$ on $0,\ldots,N$ is strictly positive. Their finite support therefore allows $u_N\ge\theta_0\psi_N$ coordinatewise for some $\theta_0>0$. Positive subsequent products preserve the inequality, so $p_\infty\ge\theta_0p_\infty'>0$.

To reach the counting coordinate, set $\mathsf z_n=u_n-p_\infty\psi_n$. Its recurrence is
\[
 \mathsf z_n=\mathsf T_n\mathsf z_{n-1}+p_\infty\varrho_n,\qquad
 \varrho_n=\mathsf T_n\psi_{n-1}-\psi_n,\qquad\norm{\varrho_n}_2\le C/n.
\]
Iterate through the last $\Delta=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps. Equations~\eqref{tcn:w:l2limit} and \eqref{tcn:w:smoothing} give
\[
 |\mathsf z_n(0)|\le C\Delta^{-3/4}n^{-1/3}
       +\frac Cn\sum_{l=0}^{\Delta-1}(l+1)^{-3/4}=O(n^{-5/6}).
\]
Together with \eqref{tcn:w:endpointphi} and $p_\infty>0$, this yields
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:vboundary}
 u_n(0)=p_\infty\sqrt{2/3}\,n^{-1/2}(1+O(n^{-1/3})).
\end{equation}

\subsection{The convergent scalar product}
The eigenvalue expansion gives
\[
 \log(\lambda_n/4)=\frac B3n^{-2/3}+\tfrac56n^{-1}+O(n^{-4/3}),
\]
since $3^{-2/3}z=B/3$. The summable remainder proves that
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:Climit}
 C_\lambda=\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{P_n}{4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{5/6}}
 \in(0,\infty),
\end{equation}
with relative error $O(n^{-1/3})$. An explicit product is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:Cproduct}
 C_\lambda=\frac{\lambda_1}{4}\ee^{-B}
 \prod_{j=2}^\infty\left[
 \frac{\lambda_j}{4}
 \ee^{-B(j^{1/3}-(j-1)^{1/3})}
 \left(\frac{j-1}{j}\right)^{5/6}\right].
\end{equation}
Each factor is $1+O(j^{-4/3})$, and finite products telescope to the quotient in \eqref{tcn:w:Climit}. The scalar projection has the absolutely convergent representation
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:cseries}
 p_\infty=1+\sum_{n=1}^\infty
       \ip{\mathsf R_n\psi_n-\psi_{n-1}}{u_{n-1}},
\end{equation}
whose summands are $O(n^{-4/3})$. Its positivity follows from the preceding restart argument, not merely from this signed series.
\begin{theorem}[Canonical amplitude; source 102]\label{tcn:w:thm:constant}
With the finite matrices, eigenvectors, and initial state specified in Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:jacobi},
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:gamma}
 \gamma_2=\sqrt{2/27}\,p_\infty C_\lambda\in(0,\infty),
\end{equation}
where \eqref{tcn:w:Cproduct} and \eqref{tcn:w:cseries} converge as stated.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorems~\ref{tcn:w:thm:leading} and \ref{tcn:w:thm:constant}]
The exact diagonal identity is
\[
 a_n=\frac{3^n n!}{3n+1}e_n(0)
     =\frac{3^n n!}{3n+1}P_nu_n(0),
\]
since $\Sigma_n(0)=1$. Combine \eqref{tcn:w:Climit} and \eqref{tcn:w:vboundary}. The exponential is $Bn^{1/3}=z(3n)^{1/3}$ and the power is $5/6-1/2-1=-2/3$. The leading constant is $\sqrt{2/3}\,p_\infty C_\lambda/3$, which is \eqref{tcn:w:gamma}.
\end{proof}
\emph{[Write phase: \eqref{tcn:w:gamma}, Part~II's \eqref{tcn:b:gamma} and Part~III's \eqref{tcn:f:gamma} at $d=2$ are three convergent representations of one number, through three gauges; none is evaluated with certified digits. Source 102's restart comparison, like Part~III's, needs no lower $\Theta$ bound.]}

\section{The all order construction and its transfer}\label{tcn:w:sec:formal}
The next results establish genuine arbitrary-order asymptotics, not just finitely verified identities. The infinite series need not converge.
\begin{theorem}[All-order expansion; source 102]\label{tcn:w:thm:allorders}
There are compatible real coefficients $c_j$, $c_0=1$, such that for every fixed $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:allorders}
 \frac{a_n}{\gamma_2 n!12^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{-2/3}}
 =\sum_{j=0}^Jc_jn^{-j/3}+O(n^{-(J+1)/3}).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, compatible $L_j$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:logseries}
 \log\frac{a_n}{\gamma_2 n!12^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{-2/3}}
 =\sum_{j=1}^JL_jn^{-j/3}+O(n^{-(J+1)/3}).
\end{equation}
The coefficients lie in $\Q[z,3^{1/3}]$ and are generated by the polynomial recursion below. The amplitude is the same $\gamma_2$ at every depth.
\end{theorem}
\emph{[Write phase: this is Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:main}; Part~II's coefficient field $\Q[B]$, with $B=3^{1/3}z$, is contained in $\Q[z,3^{1/3}]$ and is the sharper statement.]}

\subsection{An invertible polynomial equation at each depth}
Return to the one-step recurrence \eqref{tcn:w:walk}. Put
\[
 t=N^{-1/3},\quad x=(h+1)t,\quad c=2/3,\quad
 \ell=c^{2/3}z,\quad F(x)=\Ai(z+c^{1/3}x).
\]
Then $F''=(cx+\ell)F$, $F(0)=0$, and $F'(0)\ne0$. Primes here and below mean derivatives in $x$. Let $t_1=t(1-t^3)^{-1/3}$. The exact weights become
\[
 U(t,x)=\frac{3+xt^2+t^3}{3+3xt^2-3t^3},\qquad
 V(t,x)=\frac{3+xt^2+t^3}{3+xt^2-t^3}.
\]
Seek polynomial pairs $P_j,Q_j\in\Q[\ell,x]$ and scalars $\sigma_j\in\Q[\ell]$ with the gauges \eqref{tcn:f:formalansatz} and
\[
 s(t)=2+\ell t^2+\sum_{j\ge3}\sigma_jt^j,\qquad
 \mathfrak F(t,x)=\sum_{j\ge0}t^j[P_j(x)F(x)+Q_j(x)F'(x)],
\]
satisfying formally the one-step equation \eqref{tcn:f:formalequation} with these weights. The orders $t^0,t^1$ cancel, and the $t^2$ equation is the Airy equation. The new profile and scalar at order $t^k$ solve \eqref{tcn:f:solveprofile} with $c=2/3$, uniquely, by the algorithm \eqref{tcn:f:algorithm}; on polynomials of degree at most $m$ the operator $\mathcal T$ has diagonal $-2c(2j+1)$, and its inverse preserves $\Q[\ell,x]$. \emph{[Write phase: source 102 prints the system, the operator and the algorithm in full; they are literally Part~III's displays at $d=2$ and are printed once, there.]} This proves existence, uniqueness, and compatibility to every formal depth.

Each profile $f_j=P_jF+Q_jF'$ vanishes exactly at $x=0$; for $j\ge1$ its derivative there also vanishes. For example at order three,
$R_0=2$, $R_1=4x/3$, hence $\widetilde q=-2$, $\sigma_3=4/3$, $Q_1=0$, $P_1=-x^2/3$. The next order gives $\sigma_4=\ell^2/12$. These are outputs of the recurrence, not additional assumptions. \emph{[Write phase: here $R_0=2$ and $\sigma_3=4/3$, whereas Part~III at $d=2$ has $R_0=2c=4/3$ and $\sigma_3=2\eta_f=2/3$: the factorial gauge shifts the weights. $\sigma_4=\ell^2/12$ in both.]}

\subsection{A compatible scalar carrier and the endpoint normalization}
Define $h_j$ by the formal difference identity
\begin{align}
 \log\frac{s(t)}2
 &=\frac{3\ell}{2t}\bigl[1-(1-t^3)^{1/3}\bigr]-\frac23\log(1-t^3)\notag\\
 &\quad+\sum_{j\ge1}h_jt^j\bigl[1-(1-t^3)^{-j/3}\bigr].\label{tcn:w:carrier}
\end{align}
Its coefficients at $t^2,t^3$ agree with $\sigma_2=\ell$, $\sigma_3=4/3$. The new $h_j$ first has coefficient $-j/3$ at order $t^{j+3}$, so is determined uniquely. No additive integration constant is introduced; for example $h_1=\ell^2/4$.

For fixed $M\ge1$, set
\begin{align}
 \mathcal H_N^{[M]}&=2^N\ee^{(3\ell/2)N^{1/3}}N^{2/3}
                  \exp\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^Mh_jN^{-j/3}\right),\label{tcn:w:H}\\
 \mathfrak F^{[M]}(t,x)&=\sum_{j=0}^Mt^jf_j(x).\notag
\end{align}
Every carrier has leading multiplicative constant exactly one. Its exact consecutive ratio has the common expansion $s$ through at least order $t^{M+3}$. Coefficients in the carrier may use profiles beyond depth $M$; their existence has already been proved. This avoids inconsistent finite-product initialization constants.

At even time let $\eps=n^{-1/3}$, $X=(k+1/2)\eps$, so $N=2n$, $h=2k$, $t=2^{-1/3}\eps$, $x=2^{2/3}X$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:E}
 E_n^{[M]}(k)=\frac{3^{4/3}}{2^{2/3}\Ai'(z)}
       \mathcal H_{2n}^{[M]}\mathfrak F^{[M]}(2^{-1/3}\eps,2^{2/3}X).
\end{equation}
The leading bulk factor is
\[
 \frac{3^{4/3}}{\Ai'(z)}4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{2/3}
            \Ai(z+2\cdot3^{-1/3}X).
\]
At $k=0$, the Airy factor equals $\Ai'(z)3^{-1/3}\eps(1+O(\eps^2))$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:Eend}
 E_n^{[M]}(0)=3\cdot4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{1/3}(1+O(\eps)),
\end{equation}
with leading coefficient $3$ at every depth. The canonical endpoint correction series is therefore the expansion of
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:canonicalendpoint}
 \frac{E_n^{[M]}(0)}{3\cdot4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{1/3}}
                    \left(1+\frac1{3n}\right)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The last exact factor comes from \eqref{tcn:w:diagonal}, contributes from order $\eps^3$, and must be retained. All its coefficients stabilize with increasing depth.

\paragraph{The coefficient field.}
Here is why cube roots of $2$ disappear from the endpoint coefficients. Write $\mathfrak B_\ell(x)$ for the solution of $\mathfrak B''=(cx+\ell)\mathfrak B$ normalized by $\mathfrak B_\ell(0)=0$ and $\mathfrak B_\ell'(0)=1$. If $\omega^3=1$, its power-series differential equation gives
$\mathfrak B_{\omega\ell}(\omega x)=\omega\mathfrak B_\ell(x)$.
The simultaneous substitution $(t,x,\ell)\mapsto(\omega t,\omega x,\omega\ell)$ preserves the exact weights, shifts, and recurrence. Uniqueness of the polynomial recursion and gauges therefore gives, for each monomial $\ell^ix^s$, the congruences
\[
 i+s+j\equiv0\pmod3\quad\text{in }P_j,\qquad
 i+s+j\equiv1\pmod3\quad\text{in }Q_j.
\]
Likewise $i+j\equiv0\pmod3$ in $\sigma_j$ and $h_j$. Evaluation at $x=t$, division by the leading endpoint $t$, and the factor $(1+(2/3)t^3)^{-1}$ preserve invariance. Thus a term $\ell^it^j$ in the endpoint correction has $i+j\equiv0\pmod3$. Under $t=2^{-1/3}\eps$ and $\ell=(2/3)^{2/3}z$, it becomes
$2^{(2i-j)/3}3^{-2i/3}z^i\eps^j$.
Its exponent of $2$ is integral, proving the asserted field $\Q[z,3^{1/3}]$.

\subsection{Uniform residuals and all order transfer}
Fix $M$ and $C_0>0$. In the exact one-step recurrence, the residual of
$\mathcal H_N^{[M]}\mathfrak F^{[M]}(N^{-1/3},(h+1)N^{-1/3})$, divided by $\mathcal H_N^{[M]}$, is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:uniformres}
 Ct^{M+3}(1+x)^{D'}\ee^{-c_0x^{3/2}},\qquad 0\le h\le C_0\sqrt N,
\end{equation}
for large $N$. Constants may depend on the fixed depth and $C_0$; $c_0>0$ here is unrelated to the slope $c=2/3$. The proof is that of \eqref{tcn:f:oneresidual} in Part~III, with these weights: $x=O(t^{-1/2})$, the rescaled denominators stay separated from zero, finite rational Taylor expansions and polynomial--Airy envelopes control the remainders, and formal cancellation through order $t^{M+2}$ does the rest. This argument uses no convergence of an infinite expansion.

At $h=0$, the ghost predecessor has height $-1$, hence $x=0$, where every finite profile vanishes exactly before expansion. The bottom boundary is therefore exact. Eliminating the odd step gives the first row $\mathsf D_n(0)=(3n+1)/(3n)$ and $\mathsf U_n(0)=(3n+1)/(3n-1)$ already used above. The even-time residual is a bounded linear combination of the one-step residuals because the weights and carrier ratios are bounded in the cutoff region.

Choose fixed smooth $\chi$ with $0\le\chi\le1$, $\chi=1$ on $[0,1/2]$, and $\chi=0$ on $[1,\infty)$. Replace $E_n^{[M]}$ by
$\widetilde E_n^{[M]}(k)=\chi(k/\sqrt n)E_n^{[M]}(k)$.
This leaves the endpoint unchanged. In the transition region $X$ is comparable to $n^{1/6}$. Every fixed profile and needed neighbor is bounded by a polynomial in $n$ times $\ee^{-cn^{1/4}}$, after removing the scalar carrier. Every cutoff error, including change of cutoff from $n-1$ to $n$, is thus smaller than every power. Application of $\mathsf M_n$ extends the residual support at most to $k\le\sqrt n+1$.

The exact edge ratios of $\Sigma_n$ give $-\log \Sigma_n(k)=O(k^2/n+k/n)=O(1)$ on this support, so $\Sigma_n^{-1}$ is uniformly bounded there. Write
$\Theta_n=4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{2/3}$.
The squared polynomial--Airy envelope has lattice sum $O(\eps^{-1})$, whence
\[
 \norm{\widetilde E_n^{[M]}-\mathsf M_n\widetilde E_{n-1}^{[M]}}_2
            =O(\Theta_n\eps^{M+5/2}).
\]
Since $\Theta_n/P_n=O(\eps^{1/2})$ by \eqref{tcn:w:Climit}, the normalized defect obeys
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:normalizedres}
 \norm{P_n^{-1}\Sigma_n^{-1}
   (\widetilde E_n^{[M]}-\mathsf M_n\widetilde E_{n-1}^{[M]})}_2
              =O(\eps^{M+3})=O(n^{-(M+3)/3}).
\end{equation}
Increasing the depth gives an arbitrarily large forcing exponent.

Set $V_n^{[M]}=P_n^{-1}\Sigma_n^{-1}\widetilde E_n^{[M]}$. Comparing the leading bulk factor in \eqref{tcn:w:E} with the sampled unit Airy profile \eqref{tcn:w:airyf} gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:compatibility}
 \norm{V_n^{[M]}-\frac{\sqrt{27/2}}{C_\lambda}\psi_n}_2=O(\eps).
\end{equation}
In detail, the leading constant is
$3^{4/3}/(\sqrt{c_A}C_\lambda)=\sqrt{27/2}/C_\lambda$.
The finite carrier differs from its leading carrier by $1+O(\eps)$, the finitely many profile corrections have weighted $\ell^2$ norm $O(\eps)$, and the cutoff loses exponentially little. On its support,
$\Sigma_n^{-1}-1=O(\eps(1+X^2))$ in this weighted norm; this follows from the logarithmic edge-ratio estimate and the polynomial--Airy decay. Equation~\eqref{tcn:w:qclose} contributes $O(\eps^2)$ and \eqref{tcn:w:Climit} contributes $O(\eps)$. These prove \eqref{tcn:w:compatibility}.

By \eqref{tcn:w:gamma}, $\gamma_2\sqrt{27/2}/C_\lambda=p_\infty$. Consequently
$\mathsf z_n=u_n-\gamma_2V_n^{[M]}$ has norm $O(n^{-1/3})$, its Perron projection tends to zero, and it obeys the exact signed recurrence
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:forced}
 \mathsf z_n=\mathsf T_n\mathsf z_{n-1}+\varrho_n,\qquad\norm{\varrho_n}_2=O(n^{-p}),\qquad p=(M+3)/3.
\end{equation}
Neither the residual nor the approximate profiles need be positive.

\begin{lemma}[Forced product; source 102]\label{tcn:w:lem:forced}
For the exact operators $\mathsf T_n$ above, suppose \eqref{tcn:w:forced} holds with $p>4/3$,
$\norm{\mathsf z_n}_2=O(n^{-1/3})$, and $\ip{\psi_n}{\mathsf z_n}\to0$. Then
\[
 \norm{\mathsf z_n}_2=O(n^{-(p-1)}),\qquad \mathsf z_n(0)=O(n^{-1/2-(p-1)}).
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
This is Lemma~\ref{tcn:f:lem:forced} at $d=2$, with the operators of this Part, and its proof is literally the same: project as in \eqref{tcn:w:perp} and \eqref{tcn:w:increment}, now with forcing; improve the perpendicular bound by the stability fact $x_n\le(1-cn^{-2/3})x_{n-1}+O(n^{-r})\Rightarrow x_n=O(n^{-r+2/3})$; sum the scalar increments from $n+1$ to infinity using the zero limit; repeat finitely many times to reach $p-1$; and finish at the endpoint with \eqref{tcn:w:smoothing} over $\Delta=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor$ steps, which gives $O(n^{-p+1/2})+O(n^{-p+1/6})$. Source 102 adds three observations: no sign assumption enters; no logarithmic boundary is encountered, since the exponent improves to $\min(q+1/3,p-1)$ at each round; and the factor $1+C/n$ in \eqref{tcn:w:domination} is already included in the boundary estimate and introduces no additional loss.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{tcn:w:thm:allorders}]
Take $M\ge2$ so $p>4/3$. Lemma~\ref{tcn:w:lem:forced} applies to the normalized error identified above. Since $\Sigma_n(0)=1$ and
\[
 \frac{P_n}{3\cdot4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{1/3}}=O(n^{1/2}),
\]
its endpoint conclusion gives
\[
 \frac{e_n(0)-\gamma_2\widetilde E_n^{[M]}(0)}
 {3\cdot4^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{1/3}}=O(n^{-(p-1)})=O(\eps^M).
\]
The common exact factor $3^nn!/(3n+1)$ transfers this to $a_n$ without changing the relative error. Given $J$, take $M\ge\max(2,J+1)$ and expand \eqref{tcn:w:canonicalendpoint} through order $\eps^J$. Its coefficients are independent of the deeper truncation, its tail is $O(\eps^{J+1})$, and the transferred error is no larger. This proves \eqref{tcn:w:allorders}; formal logarithm gives \eqref{tcn:w:logseries}.
\end{proof}
The expansion is Poincar\'e asymptotics in one dominant Airy sector. It does not establish convergence of the infinite series or contributions smaller than every algebraic power.

\section{Exact coefficients, the total first correction and reproducible checks}\label{tcn:w:sec:coefficients}
The first six logarithmic coefficients from \eqref{tcn:w:carrier} and \eqref{tcn:w:canonicalendpoint} are
\begin{align}
 L_1&=\frac{3^{2/3}z^2}{18}=\frac{B^2}{18},&L_2&=0,\notag\\
 L_3&=-\frac19,&L_4&=-\frac{3^{2/3}z^2}{162}=-\frac{B^2}{162},\label{tcn:w:logcoeffs}\\
 L_5&=\frac{3^{1/3}z(36-z^3)}{2916}=\frac{B(108-B^3)}{8748},&
 L_6&=\frac{2z^3}{729}-\frac1{162}=\frac{2B^3}{2187}-\frac1{162}.\notag
\end{align}
For example $c_1=L_1=z^2/(2\cdot3^{4/3})$. The multiplicative expansion begins
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:firstcoeffs}
 1+\frac{3^{2/3}z^2}{18}n^{-1/3}
 +\frac{3^{1/3}z^4}{216}n^{-2/3}
 +\left(\frac{z^6}{3888}-\frac19\right)n^{-1}+O(n^{-4/3}).
\end{equation}
All remaining $c_j$ follow by formal exponentiation of $\sum L_j\eps^j$.

\begin{remark}[The fifth and sixth coefficients; write phase]\label{tcn:w:rem:L56}
$L_1,\ldots,L_4$ and $c_1,c_2,c_3$ are Part~II's \eqref{tcn:b:logforward}: $c_2=3^{1/3}z^4/216=B^4/648$ and $c_3=z^6/3888-1/9=B^6/34992-1/9$. The $B$-forms in \eqref{tcn:w:logcoeffs} are write-phase conversions ($B=3^{1/3}z$, $z^3=B^3/3$), checked symbolically. $L_5$ and $L_6$ are new: Part~II's shipped coefficient file \textup{\nolinkurl{data/69-binary-coef-coefficients.json}} stops at $L_4$. At the intake (5 October 2026) Part~II's own generator \textup{\nolinkurl{code/69-binary-coef-derive_coefficients.py}}, written independently of source 102, was rerun on a copy with \texttt{--order 9} (392 seconds); its output \texttt{logforward\_n} ends with $3^{1/3}z(36-z^3)/2916$ and $2z^3/729-1/162$, exactly $L_5$ and $L_6$. So two independent symbolic computations agree. Numerically $L_5\approx-0.056413$ and $L_6\approx-0.041240$; direct fits of the exact sequence for $n\le1500$ cannot resolve them, which is why the confirmation is symbolic. Longer ranges do resolve them: an independent fit to exact counts for $3500\le n\le7000$ gives $L_5\approx-0.05641$ (with $L_1,\ldots,L_4$ fixed) and $L_6\approx-0.0412$ (with $L_1,\ldots,L_5$ fixed), and after all six corrections the remainder decays like $n^{-7/3}$ \textup{[}added at the independent check; see the end of Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:questions}\textup{]}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The first correction for total networks}
The first correction transfers to the \emph{total} network count. Put $\gamma_T=\gamma_2\sqrt\ee/12$. The exact maximal-count identity, Theorem~\ref{tcn:w:thm:allorders}, and Proposition~\ref{tcn:w:prop:ratio-rate} give
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:totalfirst}
 \TC_n=\gamma_T(n!)^2 12^n\ee^{Bn^{1/3}}n^{-5/3}
       \left(1+L_1n^{-1/3}+O(n^{-2/3})\right).
\end{equation}
Indeed shifting $n$ to $n-1$ first changes the stretched exponential at order $n^{-2/3}$; the factorial, polynomial, and $L_1$ shifts contribute no new term at order $n^{-1/3}$. The ratio error is also $O(n^{-2/3})$. Its bound does not identify the next total-count coefficient, even though the maximal-count coefficients are available to all orders. \emph{[5 October 2026, batch~102: Part~II identifies it. Its Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:transfer} gives every finite order of $\TC_n$, through the identity \eqref{tcn:b:T1}; by \eqref{tcn:b:total-forward} the logarithmic coefficient at $n^{-2/3}$ is $-23B/72$, so the multiplicative second coefficient in \eqref{tcn:w:totalfirst} is $B^4/648-23B/72$. Source 102 could not know this; see Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:questions}.]}

As a separate shift check, the normalized successive ratio has
\begin{align}
 \frac{a_n}{12n a_{n-1}}
 &=1+\frac B3n^{-2/3}-\frac23n^{-1}
      +\frac{B^2}{27}n^{-4/3}
      -\frac{B}{9}n^{-5/3}\notag\\
 &\quad+\frac{2B^2}{243}n^{-7/3}
      -\frac{B(B^3+216)}{6561}n^{-8/3}
      +\frac{2B^3}{2187}n^{-3}+O(n^{-10/3}).\label{tcn:w:ratio}
\end{align}
The coefficients at $n^{-1/3}$ and $n^{-2}$ vanish. To justify \eqref{tcn:w:ratio}, apply the all-order theorem at a sufficiently deeper truncation before shifting and dividing. Unknown log coefficients of orders seven, eight, and nine enter the difference only at orders ten, eleven, and twelve. No arbitrary big-$O$ remainder has been differentiated. \emph{[Write phase: source 102 writes the coefficients in $z$: $3^{1/3}z/3$, $3^{2/3}z^2/27$, $-3^{1/3}z/9$, $2\cdot3^{2/3}z^2/243$, $-3^{1/3}z(z^3+72)/2187$ and $2z^3/729$; the $B$-forms were checked symbolically, and the intake checked that \eqref{tcn:w:ratio} follows exactly from $L_1,\ldots,L_6$.]}

\subsection{What is verified by finite computation}
The accompanying source package separates exact identities from analytic theorems. Rational finite-index checks compare the original row recurrence, the rotated one-step recurrence, the two-step evolution, the diagonal shift, the symmetrizer, and the positive bidiagonal factorization. These detect algebra and startup errors; they do not prove the spectral asymptotics.

The exact symbolic checks reconstruct the formal shifted polynomial--Airy recurrence through $t^9$, clear the rational denominators, and test both Airy coordinates. They check every profile's boundary and derivative gauge. A separate endpoint reconstruction solves the normalized Airy differential equation as a formal power series, evaluates the profiles at $x=t$, integrates the scalar ratio by \eqref{tcn:w:carrier}, includes the factor $(1+(2/3)t^3)^{-1}$, and matches \eqref{tcn:w:logcoeffs}, the multiplicative coefficients through order six, and \eqref{tcn:w:ratio} through order nine. These are exact finite consistency certificates. The arbitrary-depth assertion rests on the polynomial inversion and analytic transfer proved above.

All verification gates in the release checker use explicit exceptions rather than Python assertions, and the full checks are executed under ordinary Python and under \texttt{python -O}. Deliberately altered input values and coefficient tables are required to fail in both modes. The package includes the exact inputs, build and replay instructions, source provenance, and the verification summary; it does not require access to the development workspace.

\emph{[Write phase: in the repository the checker, harness, reference derivation snapshots and diagnostic script are \textup{\nolinkurl{code/102-tableaux-*}}; inputs, results, replays and provenance are \textup{\nolinkurl{data/102-tableaux-*}}; the package documentation and review are \textup{\nolinkurl{102-tableaux-checks-README.md}}, \textup{\nolinkurl{102-tableaux-checks-RESULTS.md}}, \textup{\nolinkurl{102-tableaux-diag-README.md}}, \textup{\nolinkurl{102-tableaux-SOURCES.md}} and \textup{\nolinkurl{102-tableaux-qa-REVIEW.md}}. The manuscript, its PDF, the release verifier and manifest, the generic requirements file and an in-package duplicate of \textup{\nolinkurl{endpoint_9.json}} are not shipped. The intake reran everything on copies of the delivered layout on 5 October 2026: the release verifier passed; the suite harness kills each run after 300 seconds, so its first run failed by timeout on the loaded laptop (302 seconds; the delivered record took 136 seconds in total), and a rerun with the machine quieter passed, with both baselines (4,999 gates each) and 44 of 44 rejections, in 690 seconds; direct runs of the checker passed in both modes in 114 seconds each; and \texttt{reproduce\_dp.py 100} reproduced the delivered replay. Regenerated results equal the delivered ones apart from timestamps, durations and the Python version.]}

\subsection{Numerical diagnostics without certified amplitude digits}\label{tcn:w:sec:numerics}
For context only, exact integer dynamic programming through $n=3000$, followed by high-precision floating-point evaluation, gives the following diagnostics. The second column is the uncorrected normalized count in \eqref{tcn:w:allorders}; the third removes the six displayed logarithmic corrections.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrr}
\toprule
$n$ & Uncorrected normalization & After six log corrections\\
\midrule
100 & $2.3145214818$ & $2.0226159639$\\
500 & $2.1896829266$ & $2.0226419866$\\
1000 & $2.1542867259$ & $2.0226420109$\\
2000 & $2.1265206841$ & $2.0226420146$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These values suggest a limit near $2.022642$, but no decimal of $\gamma_2$ is certified here. Exact integer recurrence evaluation does not turn an asymptotic remainder with unspecified constants into an interval bound. The table is a consistency diagnostic and is unnecessary for the proofs. The optional reproduction script and saved diagnostic values make its scope explicit. \emph{[Write phase: the uncorrected column agrees with Part~I's diagnostics at $n=500,1000,2000$, and the corrected values are consistent with Part~II's four-correction diagnostics of Section~\ref{tcn:b:sec:numerics} (2.0226420138 at $n=50000$). The saved values are \textup{\nolinkurl{data/102-tableaux-diag-saved_dp_3000.json}}.]}

\section{Inverse asymptotics}\label{tcn:w:sec:inverse}
There are two different questions: recovering the index from an exact sequence value, and finding a floor or ceiling index for an arbitrary threshold. An asymptotic approximation alone need not resolve a threshold arbitrarily close to a count. \emph{[Write phase: this section is Part~III's Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:inverse} at $d=2$, with its proofs; it is printed with its binary constants because source 102 adds a first-correction total inverse. Remark~\ref{tcn:d:rem:tai} applies, and Part~II's Section~\ref{tcn:b:sec:inversion} is stronger for totals.]}

\subsection{An eventual inverse without knowing the amplitude}
For large positive $y$, define
\begin{align}
 x_0(y)&=\frac{y}{W_0(12y/\ee)},&L_0(y)&=\log(12x_0(y)),\label{tcn:w:lambert}\\
 \hat x(y)&=x_0(y)-\frac{z(3x_0(y))^{1/3}-\tfrac16\log x_0(y)
                         +\tfrac12\log(2\pi)}{L_0(y)},\label{tcn:w:z}
\end{align}
where $W_0$ is the principal positive real branch of Lambert's $W$ function.
\begin{proposition}[source 102]\label{tcn:w:prop:inverse}
The known two-sided bound \eqref{tcn:b:known-theta} alone implies
\[
 \hat x(\log a_n)=n+O(1/\log n).
\]
Thus nearest-integer rounding recovers $n$ for all sufficiently large exact values $a_n$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
This is Proposition~\ref{tcn:f:prop:amplitudefree} with $(\mathsf p,\mathsf r)=(1,-2/3)$, $\Gamma=12$: Stirling's formula yields $\log a_n=\Phi(n)+O(1)$ with $\Phi(x)=x(\log(12x)-1)+z(3x)^{1/3}-\tfrac16\log x+\tfrac12\log(2\pi)$, and the Taylor and mean-value steps there apply verbatim. The error tends to zero, so rounding eventually succeeds.
\end{proof}
There is no effective cutoff in this assertion. The omitted $\log\gamma_2$ is a bounded constant and explains why its error is only $O(1/\log n)$.

For $n\ge2$, the original recurrence gives
$a_n\ge b_{n,n}=(3n-2)a_{n-1}>a_{n-1}$.
Also differentiating \eqref{tcn:w:z} gives $\hat x'(y)\sim1/\log x_0>0$. Therefore for a sufficiently large threshold $Y$, if
\[
 k_\le(Y)=\max\{n:a_n\le Y\},\qquad j_0=\operatorname{round}(\hat x(\log Y)),
\]
then $j_0\in\{k_\le(Y),k_\le(Y)+1\}$, and
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:threshold}
 k_\le(Y)=\begin{cases}j_0,&a_{j_0}\le Y,\\j_0-1,&a_{j_0}>Y.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Indeed $\hat x(\log Y)$ lies between $k_\le(Y)+o(1)$ and $k_\le(Y)+1+o(1)$. One exact or rigorously certified comparison selects the floor inverse. For the least index with $a_n\ge Y$, return $j_0$ if $a_{j_0}\ge Y$, and $j_0+1$ otherwise. Equality belongs to the first branch. Unverified floating-point comparison near equality is insufficient.

\subsection{Arbitrary order inverses for the tableau and maximal counts}
With the true canonically defined amplitude, put
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:FJ}
 \mathcal F_J(x)=\log\Gamma(x+1)+x\log12+z(3x)^{1/3}
       -\frac23\log x+\log\gamma_2+\sum_{j=1}^JL_jx^{-j/3}.
\end{equation}
Its derivative is eventually positive and asymptotic to $\log x$. Let $x_J(y)$ be the unique sufficiently large root of $\mathcal F_J(x)=y$. Taking logarithms in the all-order theorem and using the mean value theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:inverseall}
 x_J(\log a_n)=n+O\!\left(\frac{n^{-(J+1)/3}}{\log n}\right).
\end{equation}
This is a mathematical inverse statement with the true constant. Replacing that constant by uncertified decimal digits does not give a certified inverse. A prescribed finite number of Newton steps, $m_J=\lceil\log_2(J+4)\rceil-1$, started at \eqref{tcn:w:lambert}, suffices asymptotically, by the quadratic estimate of Part~III's \eqref{tcn:f:newton}; here
\[
 \mathcal F_J'(x)=\Psi(x+1)+\log12+\frac B3x^{-2/3}
       -\frac2{3x}-\sum_{j=1}^J\frac j3L_jx^{-j/3-1},
\]
and the elementary Stirling replacement \eqref{tcn:f:stirlinginverse}, with the same $R$, keeps the inverse order and Newton count.

For the maximal-reticulation sequence, the exact identity in \eqref{tcn:w:networkinputs} gives the model
$\mathcal M_J(x)=\log\Gamma(x+1)+\mathcal F_J(x-1)$.
Its root at $y=\log\TC_{n,n-1}$ differs from $n$ by
$O(n^{-(J+1)/3}/\log n)$, since $\mathcal M_J'(x)\sim2\log x$.
The appropriate initial Lambert approximation is
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:networklambert}
 x_0^{\rm net}(y)=\frac{y}{2W_0(\sqrt{12}\,y/(2\ee))}.
\end{equation}
The same fixed number $m_J$ of Newton steps has the desired order: the initial error remains $O(x_0^{1/3}/\log x_0)$ and the same quadratic estimate applies.

\subsection{A first correction inverse for total networks}
Let $\gamma_T=\gamma_2\sqrt\ee/12$ and define
\begin{align}
 \mathcal T_0(x)&=2\log\Gamma(x+1)+x\log12+z(3x)^{1/3}
                        -\tfrac53\log x+\log \gamma_T,\notag\\
 \mathcal T_1(x)&=\mathcal T_0(x)+L_1x^{-1/3}.\label{tcn:w:totalmodel}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{tcn:w:totalfirst} gives
$\log\TC_n=\mathcal T_1(n)+O(n^{-2/3})$. The derivative is asymptotic to $2\log n$, so the unique sufficiently large inverse satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{tcn:w:totalinverse}
 \mathcal T_1^{-1}(\log\TC_n)=n+O\!\left(\frac{n^{-2/3}}{\log n}\right).
\end{equation}
With $y=\log(\text{target})$, starting at \eqref{tcn:w:networklambert}, two exact Newton steps for $\mathcal T_1(x)=y$ suffice for this order. The initial error is $O(x_0^{1/3}/\log x_0)$, while the first and second Newton root errors are respectively $O(x_0^{-1/3}/\log^3x_0)$ and $O(x_0^{-5/3}/\log^7x_0)$, by the same quadratic estimate as above. The second is smaller than the model error in \eqref{tcn:w:totalinverse}.

For comparison, using only the leading model gives
$\mathcal T_0^{-1}(\log\TC_n)=n+O(n^{-1/3}/\log n)$.
An explicit simplified one-step expression, with $x_0=x_0^{\rm net}(y)$, is
\[
 \hat x_T(y)=x_0-
 \frac{z(3x_0)^{1/3}-\tfrac23\log x_0+\log(2\pi \gamma_T)}
 {2\log(\sqrt{12}\,x_0)}.
\]
Taylor's theorem gives $\hat x_T(\log\TC_n)=n+O(n^{-1/3}/\log n)$. Its analytic root error is $O(x_0^{-1/3}/\log^2x_0)$; omitting $L_1$ limits its model accuracy. It is not an implementation of the sharper two-step estimate. If $\log \gamma_T$ is omitted as well, only $O(1/\log n)$ is asserted.

The leading equivalent implies $\TC_{n+1}/\TC_n\sim12n^2$, hence eventual monotonicity. The same two-candidate threshold rule applies, but still needs an exact or certified comparison and an eventual cutoff. The constants must be evaluated accurately enough for any numerical use. No all-order total-count inverse is claimed; more terms require more than the ratio bound proved here. \emph{[Write phase: Part~II's \eqref{tcn:b:ht} and \eqref{tcn:b:inverse-total} already include the $n^{-2/3}$ and $n^{-1}$ total corrections, and its transfer gives every order; \eqref{tcn:w:totalinverse} is the identity-free counterpart. $\hat x_T$ is exactly the truncation displayed in Part~II's \eqref{tcn:b:inverse-total}: there $x=x_0^{\rm net}(y)$, $\mathsf s=2\log(2\sqrt3\,x)$ and $C_T=\log(2\pi\gamma_T)$.]}

\section{Further questions and research (Part IV)}\label{tcn:w:sec:questions}
Source 102's closing section, ``Further questions and boundaries of the result'', is printed first, with dated notes; the write-phase items follow. No claim of source 102 was found to be wrong.

\paragraph{Certified amplitude computation.}
The product and scalar series in \eqref{tcn:w:Cproduct} and \eqref{tcn:w:cseries} give a route to numerical enclosure. Effective constants in the spectral, drift, projection, and smoothing estimates would be needed to bound the tails explicitly. Interval computations of finite matrices could then certify digits of $\gamma_2$ and effective inverse cutoffs. The present proof supplies existence and qualitative rates, not such numerical enclosures. \emph{[Write phase: still open; Part~I's question~2 (Section~\ref{tcn:d:sec:status}).]}

\paragraph{Corrections for total networks.}
The quantitative ratio and total-variation bound transfer the first correction. Identifying the total-count coefficient at order $n^{-2/3}$, and then an all-order total-count expansion, requires sharper information on the free-root-child counts $F_{n,r}$ or their aggregate weights. A big-$O$ bound for their contribution cannot determine those coefficients. \emph{[5 October 2026, batch~102: answered in the repository. Part~II's Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:transfer} gives the all-order total-count expansion, and \eqref{tcn:b:total-forward} gives the coefficient asked for: $-23B/72$ in logarithmic form, $B^4/648-23B/72$ in the multiplicative form of \eqref{tcn:w:totalfirst}. It uses the Pons--Batle identity \eqref{tcn:b:T1}, proved in \cite{LL2026}, which source 102 cites but does not use, rather than information on $F_{n,r}$. Whether the free-root-child route of Proposition~\ref{tcn:w:prop:ratio-rate} can itself be pushed to all orders remains open; see item~1 below.]}

\paragraph{Beyond all algebraic orders.}
The Airy spectrum suggests additional decaying components, but a transseries would require their separate amplitudes, interactions, and uniform remainders. Neither the all-depth polynomial algorithm nor a finite numerical residual proves an exponentially small sector.

\paragraph{Other network regimes.}
Fixed, sparse, and maximal reticulation counts occupy different asymptotic regimes. The exact all-order statement here is for the maximal diagonal through its word/tableau normalization. A uniform bridge to sparse-reticulation results would need a separate two-parameter analysis.

\paragraph{Scope of the proof and source package.}
The leading proof includes its own form compactness, localization, dimension-change treatment, boundary kernel, positivity argument, and amplitude product. The all-order proof includes its own forced-product lemma and does not require a companion relaxed-tree article. Exact finite checks are reproducibility safeguards, not a machine verification of the analytic proof. The finite literature inspection supports precise attribution of the prior scale and ratio theorem, but is not a claim of exhaustive novelty. \emph{[Write phase: the ``companion relaxed-tree article'' is presumably the relaxed-tree manuscript of the same delivery series, bundle Report~100, placed as Part~V of the sibling report \textup{\nolinkurl{../a082161-airy-amplitudes}} by \texttt{6ab1f1979}; the two share the lemma family of Parts~I--IV.]}

\paragraph{Write-phase items.}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{An identity-free all-order transfer.} Proposition~\ref{tcn:w:prop:ratio-rate} obtains $\mathcal R_{n-1}=\sqrt\ee(1+O(n^{-2/3}))$ from Lemmas 3.17--3.20 of \cite{Chang2024} alone. Whether expansions of the free-root-child ratios $\mathfrak f_k$ could give the all-order transfer of Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:transfer} without the identity \eqref{tcn:b:T1} is open; such a route might extend to $d\ge3$, where no analogue of \eqref{tcn:b:T1} is known (Part~III, Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:questions}, item~2). (Write phase.)
\item \emph{Certified digits.} The diagnostics $2.0226420146$ (here) and $2.02264201$ (Part~II) are uncertified; see the first paragraph. (Sources 102 and 69.) \textup{[}Added at the independent check: a fit to exact counts for $3500\le n\le7000$ with the six coefficients of \eqref{tcn:w:logcoeffs} fixed gives $\gamma_2\approx2.0226420155$, likewise uncertified; the value $2.0226420146$ at $n=2000$ in Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:numerics} lies below it by about the size of the remaining $n^{-7/3}$ term.\textup{]}
\item \emph{The next logarithmic coefficients.} $L_7,L_8,L_9$ enter \eqref{tcn:w:ratio} only beyond $n^{-3}$; they are computable by the same algorithm (Part~II's generator at higher order) but were not computed. (Write phase.)
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Independent check of the write (dated note, 5~October 2026).}
An independent adversarial check of Parts~III and~IV, made by the intake after the write (\texttt{7e85b5a0c}), reread the fifteen items of the write that carry a proof, a conversion or a numerical claim. In Part~III these are Remark~\ref{tcn:f:rem:main} with \eqref{tcn:f:c1} (the $B$-form of $C_{d,1}$, its values at $d=2,3$, its sign and its field); the write's notes on \eqref{tcn:f:b2} (the bracket, its sign change and $E_{d,1}$, $E_{d,2}$), on \eqref{tcn:f:ternarycoeffs} with $C_{3,3}$ and \eqref{tcn:f:ternarylog3}, on \eqref{tcn:f:h1} ($h_2$ and the values at $d=2$), on \eqref{tcn:f:lambda} and on \eqref{tcn:f:lambert}; Remarks~\ref{tcn:f:rem:naive} and~\ref{tcn:f:rem:reading}; the identification of Part~III's $\gamma_d$ with the host's at the end of Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:notation}; and the limit $C_{d,1}\to-19z^2/90$ in item~4 of Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:questions}. In Part~IV they are Remarks~\ref{tcn:w:rem:tv} and~\ref{tcn:w:rem:L56} and the write's notes on \eqref{tcn:w:ratio}, \eqref{tcn:w:totalfirst} and Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:inverse}. It found all fifteen items valid, no counterexample, and no gap in any proof chain. Three refinements were adopted where they stand. Remark~\ref{tcn:w:rem:tv} cited the expansion of $\mathcal R_n$ to \eqref{tcn:b:D8}, which is the expansion of $d_{\mathrm{TV}}$; the citation now names the proof of Theorem~\ref{tcn:b:thm:tv}, where that expansion is displayed. Remark~\ref{tcn:w:rem:L56} now records the confirmation of $L_5$ and $L_6$ by exact counts. Item~6 of Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:questions} and item~2 of the write-phase items above now carry the check's uncertified numerical by-products. No statement or number changed.

The check used neither the delivered programs nor the write's. Its own exact big-integer implementation of the recurrence \eqref{tcn:d:brec} reproduces all 321 terms ($0\le n\le320$) of the OEIS b-file of A213863, fetched on 5~October 2026, and the start $1,1,25,2305$ of the diagonal at $d=3$ printed in Part~I; it was run to $n=7000$ at $d=2$, to $n=2000$ at $d=3$ and to $n=1600$ at $d=4$ and $d=5$. In exact SymPy arithmetic it re-derived the conversions of the fifteen items (more than eighty checks), among them the following. First, $8(d+1)^2-27(d-1)^2=-19d^2+70d-19$, with roots $(35\pm12\sqrt6)/19\approx0.295$ and $3.389$, so $C_{d,1}$ is positive at $d=2,3$ and negative for every integer $d\ge4$ (also checked one by one for $4\le d\le10^4$). Second, $27(d-1)^2-(d+1)^3=(d-2)(-d^2+22d-13)$, whose quadratic factor has roots $11\pm6\sqrt3\approx0.608$ and $21.392$, so the bracket of \eqref{tcn:f:b2} vanishes at $d=2$, is positive for $3\le d\le21$ and is negative for $d\ge22$ (witnesses $10800>10648$ at $d=21$ and $11907<12167$ at $d=22$). Third, $h_2=-\ell(27c^3-36c^2+16)/(108c^2)$, from the carrier identity \eqref{tcn:f:carrieridentity} through $t^5$. Fourth, the $B$-forms of $L_5$, $L_6$, $c_2$ and $c_3$. Fifth, \eqref{tcn:w:ratio}, by expanding the shifted quotient with $L_7$, $L_8$, $L_9$ symbolic; they do not enter through $n^{-3}$. For Remark~\ref{tcn:w:rem:tv} it confirmed $\tfrac32(1/\mathcal R-\ee^{-1/2})=\tfrac3{2\mathcal R}\cdot(\sqrt\ee-\mathcal R)/\sqrt\ee$, $3/(2\sqrt\ee)=0.9097959896\ldots$, and the leaf index ($\mathcal R_{n-1}$ in source 102's count). For Remark~\ref{tcn:f:rem:reading} it confirmed the gamma-ratio product (at $m=40$, to 30~digits) and $\sum_{k\ge1}1/(2^{k-1}k!)=2(\ee^{1/2}-1)$; the two facts read from \cite{Chang2024} remain unchecked, as the remark says.

The numerical tests are least-squares fits of the logarithmic remainder in 70-digit arithmetic, with the amplitude $\log\gamma_d$ unknown, free powers $n^{-j/3}$, and both the window $[n_{\mathrm{lo}},N]$ and the number of free powers varied. At $d=3,4,5$, with nothing fixed, the fitted first coefficients are $0.1205267$, $-0.1468634$ and $-0.3183762$, against $0.1205266$, $-0.1468635$ and $-0.3183762$ from \eqref{tcn:f:c1}; so exact counts confirm the negative sign of $C_{4,1}$ and $C_{5,1}$. With $C_{d,1}$ fixed, the fitted second coefficients are $-0.150019$, $-0.161536$ and $-0.148692$, against $-0.1500192$, $-0.1615366$ and $-0.1486925$ from \eqref{tcn:f:b2}. With both fixed, $L_{3,3}$ fits as $-0.24010$, against $-0.2401002$ from \eqref{tcn:f:ternarylog3}. The fits discriminate: perturbing $C_{d,1}$ by $0.1\%$ moves the fitted second coefficient by $4$--$10\%$, and perturbing $L_{d,2}$ by $1\%$ moves the fitted third by $23$--$42\%$. The fitted amplitudes $\gamma_3\approx3.784368124$, $\gamma_4\approx3.478351222$ and $\gamma_5\approx2.38475472$ agree with the intake's values in Section~\ref{tcn:f:sec:reproducibility}. At $d=2$, on the window $3500\le n\le7000$ with five to seven free powers, the fit gives $L_5=-0.056413$, $-0.056411$ and $-0.056417$ with $L_1,\ldots,L_4$ fixed, against $-0.0564125$, and $L_6=-0.041243$, $-0.041225$ and $-0.041291$ with $L_1,\ldots,L_5$ fixed, against $-0.0412396$. The sharpest test is the decay of the remainder after all six corrections. The remainder at $n=1000$ is $5.08$ times that at $n=2000$ and $13.1$ times that at $n=3000$, against $2^{7/3}=5.04$ and $3^{7/3}=12.98$ for a decay like $n^{-7/3}$; a wrong $L_5$ would leave $n^{-5/3}$ (factor $3.17$), a wrong $L_6$ would leave $n^{-2}$ (factor $4$). The implied $\gamma_2\approx2.0226420155$ is uncertified, and $L_7$, roughly $-0.02$, is not resolved. Fits for $n\le3000$ pin $L_5$ only to about $20\%$ and do not pin $L_6$, in line with what Remark~\ref{tcn:w:rem:L56} says of $n\le1500$. The table of Section~\ref{tcn:w:sec:numerics} is reproduced to every printed digit. Part~II's four-correction value $2.0226420138$ at $n=50000$ (Section~\ref{tcn:b:sec:numerics}) equals $\gamma_2(1+L_5n^{-5/3})$ to about $10^{-12}$, which corroborates $L_5$ independently. This was a careful reading with numerical tests, not a formal verification.
